**Failure scenario.** Configure forEach {collection: '$operationdata.items'}, then removePropertiesFromOperationData {propertyNames:['items']} (or clearOperationData, or a setGlobalData that replaces a `$globaldata` list with a shorter one), then endForEach. Start with items [1,2,3]: iteration 1 sees 1, iterations 2 and 3 see currentItem undefined and still run the body.

**Evidence.**

```ts
const resolvedCollection =
    typeof collection === 'string'
      ? (resolveExternalPropertyChain(
          operationData,
          this,
          collection as ExternalProperty
        ) as any[])
      : collection;
...
  if (resolvedCollection?.length && this.loopIndex !== undefined) {
    this.currentItem = resolvedCollection[this.loopIndex];
  }
```

**Fix.** Take a snapshot when the loop starts, keyed by the loop scope: - In forEach, resolve `collection` only when `this.loopIndex === undefined`. - Store the array (a shallow copy, so in-place changes to the source array are also ignored) in a module-level `WeakMap<IOperationScope, unknown[]>`, or on a non-enumerable scope field. - On later passes, read `currentItem` from that snapshot. - Delete the entry when the loop ends: in endForEach, or wherever the scope is popped.  Alternatively, if per-iteration re-resolution is intended, document it and throw a named error when the re-resolved collection is missing or shorter than `loopIndex`, instead of passing undefined to the body.  Add an integration test in src/test/integration/for-each-loop.spec.ts with a body that removes the source property.

Original suggestion: Resolve the collection once, when `this.loopIndex === undefined`, and store it on the loop scope (for example a private `loopCollection` field, or a WeakMap keyed by scope like `_loopOperationData`). Later iterations should read that stored array. Add a spec in for-each-loop.spec.ts where the body removes the source property.

**Verifier.** The described behaviour is real, but it only happens when a loop body changes the collection it is looping over, so it is low severity, not medium.  How the code behaves: - In src/operation/for-each.ts (lines 36-43), forEach resolves `collection` on every pass, because the action jumps back to the forEach operation for each iteration. - `loopLength` is set only on the first pass (line 60). endForEach (src/operation/end-for-each.ts) loops while `loopIndex < loopLength` and never checks the collection again. - Action caches only the configured data in `_loopOperationData` (src/action/action.ts). That is the `collection` string, not the array it resolves to. So later passes re-resolve the chain against operation data the body may have changed.  Guards I checked, and why they do not stop it: - resolveExternalPropertyChain → getPropertyChainValue throws only when a parent value in the chain is null or undefined. For the single-segment chain `items`, the parent is operationData itself, so a missing `items` returns undefined without an error. - The "Expected resolved collection ... array" check (lines 45-53) lets null and undefined through. - Line 72 (`resolvedCollection?.length`) is falsy, so `currentItem` is not set. endForEach had already deleted it, so it stays undefined while the body still runs.  Probe: I added a temporary spec under src/test/integration and ran it with vitest, then deleted it. Setup: an operation that sets `items: [1,2,3]`, then forEach `{collection: '$operationdata.items'}`, a recorder operation, removePropertiesFromOperationData `{propertyNames: ['items']}`, and endForEach. Recorded currentItem values: [1, undefined, undefined]. This matches the report.  No existing test covers this. The tests in src/test/integration/for-each-loop.spec.ts and src/test/unit/operation/for-each.spec.ts use literal arrays or collections that do not change.  Why low and not medium: - It can be triggered from a valid configuration, but only by removing or replacing the source collection inside its own loop body. That is the same as changing a list while looping over it, an unusual and arguably wrong pattern. - Typical configurations do not do this. - The result is a silently undefined currentItem. It does not corrupt engine state or crash. - The same design has a milder side effect: if the body pushes onto the same array, the new items are not visited, because loopLength is fixed.

**Repro test.** `given forEach over '$operationdata.items' = [1,2,3] and a loop body that removes 'items' from operation data, when the action runs, then currentItem is 1, 2, 3 on the three iterations (today it is 1, undefined, undefined)`

### 30. [low] customFunction in a when block inside a loop gets no currentItem/loopIndex on this

[custom-function.ts:54](src/operation/custom-function.ts#L54) · area: operations-a · reported severity: medium · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** `customFunction` calls the user function with `this` set to the operation scope. Inside a `when` block that scope is the child scope that `Action._pushScope` creates. It forwards only currentIndex, eventbus and operations, so `this.currentItem`, `this.loopIndex` and `this.variables` are undefined there, although they are set when the same function runs directly in the loop body. `$scope.` chains get around this by walking `parent` (getScopeChainValue), but direct `this` access does not. A probe confirmed it: forEach [1,2] > when '1==1' > customFunction pushing this.currentItem collected [undefined, undefined].

**Failure scenario.** Operations: forEach {collection:[1,2]}, when {expression:'1==1'}, customFunction {systemName:'fn'} where fn reads this.currentItem, endWhen, endForEach. fn gets undefined on every iteration. Remove the when and it gets 1 and 2.

**Evidence.**

```ts
const result = func.apply(this, [operationData]);

// action.ts _pushScope:
const newScope = {
      get currentIndex() {...},
      get eventbus() {...},
      get operations() {...},
      parent: parentScope,
    };
```

**Fix.** Do not change `_pushScope`. Making when-scopes inherit loopIndex/loopLength (via Object.create or getters) makes `isLoopScope` in loop-jump.ts treat when-scopes as loop scopes, which breaks break/continue unwinding. With Object.create, a nested forEach's first-iteration check (`this.loopIndex === undefined`) would also read the outer loop's index. Fix it locally in customFunction instead. Before `func.apply`, build a read view with a Proxy over `this`: `get` returns the scope's own value when `prop in target`, otherwise it walks `parent` (the same rule as getScopeChainValue); `set` and `deleteProperty` go to the real scope. Pass that view as `this`. Add a regression spec: forEach [1,2] > when > customFunction reading this.currentItem / this.loopIndex / this.variables, expecting [1,2] and [0,1]. Also update the customFunction TypeDoc to state what `this` exposes. A docs-only alternative is to say that `this` is the innermost block scope and that loop state should be passed in with `$scope.currentItem` via setOperationData.

Original suggestion: Make the when-scope inherit its loop state. Either create it with `Object.create(parentScope)` plus own `parent`/`whenEvaluation`/`newIndex` fields so reads fall through to the parent, or add getters for currentItem/loopIndex/loopLength/variables in `_pushScope`. As a smaller step, have customFunction pass a scope view that resolves these through the parent chain. Add a regression spec.

**Verifier.** I traced the scenario through the code and ran it, and it holds. In Action.executeOperation (src/action/action.ts ~155-169), a `when` calls `_pushScope(scopeStack, scope)`. The new scope proxies only currentIndex (get/set), eventbus and operations, and adds `parent`. Every later operation is invoked with `operationInfo.instance.call(scope, ...)`. forEach writes currentItem/loopIndex/loopLength onto its own pushed loop scope, so inside a nested when the active scope has none of them. customFunction (src/operation/custom-function.ts:54) does `func.apply(this, [operationData])`, which passes that bare when-scope. Only `$scope.` chains fall through to the parent: getScopeChainValue in resolve-external-property-chain.ts walks `parent`, and its doc comment admits that child scopes do not proxy `variables`. Nothing in customFunction or Action fills the gap.  I ran the exact scenario as a temporary spec: forEach [1,2] > when '1==1' > customFunction recording [this.currentItem, this.loopIndex] > endWhen > endForEach. It recorded [[undefined, undefined], [undefined, undefined]]. I deleted the probe afterwards. No existing test covers `this` inside a when-in-loop for customFunction. custom-function.spec.ts only tests lookup errors and the promise path. action.spec.ts checks this.currentItem only directly in a loop body.  Mitigating factors that lower the severity: - customFunction only works with a custom resource importer, since the default importer exposes no user functions. - The `this` contract for custom functions is not documented. The TypeDoc says the function gets operation data and the eventbus. - There is a working path today: `setOperationData` / `$scope.currentItem` resolve through the parent chain, so the value can be passed in via operation data. - Nothing corrupts. The function just reads undefined.  So: low, not medium.  The suggested fix is also dangerous. Making when-scopes inherit loopIndex/loopLength, through `Object.create(parentScope)` or getters in `_pushScope`, would make `isLoopScope` in src/operation/helper/loop-jump.ts return true for when-scopes. break/continue would then stop unwinding through endWhen correctly. With Object.create, a nested forEach's `this.loopIndex === undefined` first-iteration check in for-each.ts would also read the outer loop's index and skip its own initialisation. Any fix must leave _pushScope's own-property layout alone.

**Repro test.** `given a customFunction inside a when block inside a forEach over [1,2], when the action starts, then the function sees this.currentItem 1 and 2 and this.loopIndex 0 and 1`

### 31. [low] when treats unrecognised operands (true, false, null, @var, typos) as literal strings

[when.ts:134](src/operation/when.ts#L134) · area: operations-a · reported severity: medium · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** `resolveOperand` returns any token that is not quoted, not numeric and not a known `$` prefix unchanged, as a string. The JSDoc documents `@foo` as variable syntax, but it is never resolved. Boolean and null literals compare as the strings 'true', 'false' and 'null'. A misspelt prefix such as `$operatondata.x` becomes a literal instead of an error. A probe confirmed all three: `$operationdata.flag==true` with flag=true gives false, `@foo==1` with variables.foo=1 gives false, and `$operatondata.x=='$operatondata.x'` gives true. `parseFloat` also accepts partial numbers ('10px' becomes 10).

**Failure scenario.** expression '$operationdata.isVisible==true' with isVisible true: whenEvaluation is false, so the branch is always skipped and no error is reported. expression '@count>3': always false.

**Evidence.**

```ts
const tokenNr = parseFloat(token);
  return (
    Number.isNaN(tokenNr)
      ? resolveExternalPropertyChain(
          operationData,
          operationScope,
          token as ExternalProperty
        )
      : tokenNr
  ) as TValue;
...
 * @foo: for a variable value
```

**Fix.** In resolveOperand, after the quoted-string check: - Accept `true`, `false` and `null` explicitly. - Treat a token as a number only if it matches /^-?\d+(\.\d+)?([eE][-+]?\d+)?$/, then use Number(token). - Resolve the token as a chain only if isExternalProperty(token). - Otherwise throw `when: unknown operand '<token>' in expression '<expression>'` (pass the expression in).  Separately, delete the stale `@foo: for a variable value` JSDoc line, since #49 removed that syntax in favour of `$scope.variables.foo`. Add tests for boolean literals, an unknown prefix and a partial number.

Original suggestion: Accept `true`, `false` and `null` explicitly. Use `Number(token)` with a full-token numeric regex instead of parseFloat. Either implement `@name` as `$scope.variables.name` or remove it from the docs. Throw `when: unknown operand '<token>' in expression '<expr>'` for anything else, which matches the changelog's 'throws on an invalid expression'.

**Verifier.** I traced the code path and it does what the report says. In src/operation/when.ts, resolveOperand (lines 124-144) unquotes `'...'` tokens and otherwise calls parseFloat. If that gives NaN it calls resolveExternalPropertyChain (src/operation/helper/resolve-external-property-chain.ts). That function only resolves strings that isExternalProperty recognises, meaning the `$operationdata.`, `$globaldata.` and `$scope.` prefixes. Anything else comes back unchanged as a string. So `true` becomes the string 'true', `$operationdata.flag==true` evaluates `true === 'true'` and gives false, and `$operatondata.x` becomes a string literal with no error. splitExpression only throws when the operator is missing or an operand is empty (that is what the changelog's 'throws on an invalid expression' covers). parseFloat('10px') returns 10.  Several mitigating facts mean this should not be rated medium as reported: (1) The `@foo` variable syntax was deliberately removed in commit 1d823a12 (#49), which dropped `isVariableName = value.startsWith('@')` and replaced the example with `$scope.variables.foo`. The `@foo: for a variable value` line at when.ts:41 is a leftover JSDoc line, not a lost feature. It is also missing from the generated metadata (src/operation/metadata/when.ts), which is what editors and the README show, because the generator reads `@foo` as a doc tag. So that part is a stale doc only. (2) Boolean, null, typo and `10px` operands are not in the documented grammar. TValue/TExpression only allow a quoted string, a number, or `$operationdata.`/`$globaldata.`/`$scope.` chains. None of the scenarios can come from a configuration that follows the documented grammar. (3) The JSON schema types `expression` as a plain string, so nothing validates it earlier, and no test in src/test/unit/operation/when.spec.ts covers unknown operands. The real defect is therefore a missing fail-fast check: invalid operands are silently treated as literals, which breaks CLAUDE.md's 'invalid configuration must fail fast' rule and makes a `when` branch silently do nothing. A related feature gap: there is no way to compare against a boolean at all.  Corrected severity is low: it is real, but it only affects expressions outside the documented grammar.

**Repro test.** `given operationData {flag: true} and expression '$operationdata.flag==true', when the when operation is applied, then whenEvaluation is true (fails today: 'true' is compared as a string, and an unknown operand such as '$operatondata.x' does not throw)`

### 32. [low] DOMEventListenerController turns action failures into unhandled promise rejections

[dom-event-listener-controller.ts:103](src/controllers/dom-event-listener-controller.ts#L103) · area: controllers · reported severity: medium · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** The jQuery event handler starts the async action chain without awaiting it or attaching a rejection handler, and each recursive step is not awaited either. A failing action rejects with nothing listening. It is never reported through the engine/eventbus error path (the changelog fixed this for ActionRegistryEventbusListener only), and the remaining actions in the list are skipped silently.

**Failure scenario.** Configure a DOMEventListenerController on a button with actions ['a','b'], where action 'a' has an operation that throws (e.g. an unknown selector with a strict operation). Click the button: the browser logs 'Uncaught (in promise)' with no action or controller context, 'engine-error' is never broadcast, and action 'b' never runs.

**Evidence.**

```ts
this._executeAction(this.actionInstanceInfos, actionOperationData, 0);
...
      const resultOperationData = await method(operationData);
      this._executeAction(
        actions,
        Object.assign(operationData, resultOperationData),
        ++idx
      );
```

**Fix.** Make `_executeAction` a plain loop: `for (const info of actions) { const result = await (info.start ? info.action.start(data) : info.action.end(data)); Object.assign(data, result); }`. This keeps every step on one awaited promise. Then attach a handler in `_eventHandler`: `this._executeActions(...).catch(error => this._eventbus?.broadcast(...))`. Store the eventbus in attach and clear it in detach. Either route the error to the same handler-error reporting that Eventbus uses for listener failures, or document widening 'engine-error' to cover controller-started actions. Keep the current stop-on-first-failure behaviour, because later actions consume the earlier actions' operation data.

Original suggestion: Return/await the recursive call (or use a loop) and catch at the handler: `this._executeActions(...).catch(error => eventbus.broadcast('engine-error', [...]))`, or route it through the same reporting the ActionRegistryEventbusListener uses. Store the eventbus at attach for this.

**Verifier.** I traced the code and the core claim holds. In src/controllers/dom-event-listener-controller.ts, `_eventHandler` (line 103) calls the async `_executeAction` and drops the promise it returns. `_executeAction` does `await method(operationData)`, and `method` is `Action.start` or `EndableAction.end`. Both of those catch the error, call console.error and throw it again (src/action/action.ts, src/action/endable-action.ts). When action 'a' fails, `_executeAction` rejects and nothing is listening, so the browser reports an unhandled rejection. The recursive call at line 118 is not awaited or returned either. A failure in action 'b' or any later action ends up in a separate floating promise, so a catch added only at the top would still miss it. The controller has no other guard. It does not keep the eventbus after attach, and src/test/unit/controllers/DOMEventListenerController.spec.ts has no test for a rejecting action. The listener that the changelog fix covered (src/eventbus/actionregistry-eventbus-listener.ts) now returns an allSettled promise that Eventbus._reportError picks up. The DOM path got no such fix.  Some of the report is overstated, so I lowered the severity: (1) The failure is not silent and does have context. Action.start logs "Error in action start '<name>'" plus the error, and the error is tagged with operationSystemName/operationIndex. (2) 'engine-error' is documented (src/eventbus/events/engine-error.ts) as covering only timeline actions that the engine starts or ends itself. Not broadcasting it here matches the documented scope. The real gap is that there is no reporting path at all (no Eventbus._reportError, no onError hook) and the rejection is unhandled. (3) Skipping action 'b' after 'a' fails fits the sequential chain. Each action's output data is merged into the next action's input, so stopping is a defensible choice, not a defect in itself. Engine state is not corrupted. The visible effects are an extra "Uncaught (in promise)" message and no programmatic error hook. That makes the severity low.

**Repro test.** `given a DOMEventListenerController attached with actions ['a','b'] where action a's start rejects, when the element's click event fires, then the rejection is reported through the eventbus (no unhandled rejection) and action b's start is not called`

### 33. [low] Routing popstate with null state ignores location.hash, jumps to first entry

[routing-controller.ts:67](src/controllers/routing-controller.ts#L67) · area: controllers · reported severity: medium · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** A popstate caused by a hash change (an in-page <a href="#/chapter2"> link, the user editing the hash, or the host page's own history entries) has `event.state === null`. _findNavigation(undefined) then falls back to navigation[0], so the controller requests the first entry instead of the one in location.hash. It also hijacks popstate events the host page created with its own unrelated state.

**Failure scenario.** At #/nav1, the user clicks an in-page link to #/nav2. popstate fires with state null, and the controller broadcasts highlight-navigation [0] and request-video-url [0, 0, true] (nav1), not nav2. The existing test 'popstate without state' passes only because its URL hash is #/nav1.

**Evidence.**

```ts
private _handlePopstate(event: any) {
    const nav = this._findNavigation(event.state?.navigationId);
...
  private _findNavigation(navId: unknown): any | undefined {
    if (typeof navId === 'string' && Object.hasOwn(this.navLookup, navId)) {
      return this.navLookup[navId];
    }
    return this.navigation?.[0];
```

**Fix.** In `_handlePopstate`: - When `event.state` is a non-null object with no string `navigationId`, return early. That state belongs to the host page. - When `event.state` is null, resolve the entry from the URL: `const navId = event.state?.navigationId ?? this._getQueryVariable(0)` and `const position = event.state ? event.state.position : this._getQueryVariable(1)`. - Keep the navigation[0] fallback only for an empty hash. That is the "back to the entry before attach" case the existing test covers.  Add tests for: - null state with hash `#/nav2/10`, expecting `request-video-url [1, 10, true]` - a host state `{foo: 1}`, expecting no broadcast

Original suggestion: When event.state has no navigationId, read it from location.hash with _getQueryVariable(0)/(1). Ignore popstate events whose state is non-null but has no navigationId, since those belong to the host page.

**Verifier.** I traced the code in src/controllers/routing-controller.ts and the failure is real. `_handlePopstate` (line 66) passes `event.state?.navigationId` to `_findNavigation` (line 84). That function returns `navLookup[id]` only when the id is a string that is an own key; in every other case it returns `this.navigation[0]`. `location.hash` is read only in `attach()` (lines 47-56), never on popstate. So whenever a popstate arrives with state null, or with a state object that lacks `navigationId`, the controller broadcasts `highlight-navigation [nav0.videoUrlIndex]` and `request-video-url [nav0.videoUrlIndex, 0, true]`, whatever the URL says. Nothing else guards this: `attach` listens to popstate with no filter.  The reasons I looked for to refute it do not hold: 1. **Intended default.** The original main-branch code also defaulted to navigation[0] on null state, and the test 'should handle popstate without state (default to first navigation)' locks that in. That default is only correct for one case: going back to the entry from before attach when the page loaded with no hash, which is the entry attach pushed `#/nav[0]` on top of. 2. **The controller's own flow hits the bug, no host links needed.** Load the page at `#/nav2/10`. `attach` broadcasts `request-video-url` with isHistoryRequest=true, so `_handleBeforeRequestVideoUrl` pushes nothing and the first history entry keeps state null. The user then moves to another video. `_pushState` sees `history.state` null, takes the else branch and pushes a new entry. When the user presses Back, popstate fires with state null while `location.hash` is `#/nav2/10`, and the controller jumps to nav1 at 0. 3. **In-page links and host entries.** Fragment links like `<a href="#/nav2">` also fire popstate with null state, and the host page's own state objects (non-null, no `navigationId`) are hijacked the same way. 4. **Test coverage.** The existing null-state test passes only because `beforeEach` sets the URL to `/#/nav1`, which equals navigation[0].  **Mitigating factor:** the class JSDoc says "This is a work in progress, do not use this yet please....". So this is an experimental controller with a disclaimer, even though it is exported and has generated metadata. It is also an existing behaviour carried over from the original code, not a regression. For those reasons I lower the severity from medium to low.

**Repro test.** `given the URL hash is #/nav2/10 and an attached RoutingController, when a popstate event fires with null state, then request-video-url is broadcast for nav2 at position 10 instead of the first navigation entry`

### 34. [low] createEngine overwrites the factory-wide _actionsLookup and keeps it after destroy

[engine-factory.ts:197](src/engine-factory.ts#L197) · area: lens-lifecycle-memory · reported severity: medium · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** The 'request-action' responder reads a single factory field that each createEngine replaces. With two live engines, controllers and operations of the first engine look up actions in the second engine's map. After the per-engine destroy() the map still references the destroyed engine's whole action graph until the next createEngine or factory.destroy().

**Failure scenario.** Engine A has a timeline action at 5s that adds a DOMEventListenerController with actions ['ShowInfo']. Engine B is created from the same factory at page load. At 5s A's controller requests 'ShowInfo' from B's lookup: it logs 'Unknown action: ShowInfo' and the click does nothing, or it runs B's action if B also defines that name. Separately, after `await resultA.destroy()` the factory still retains all of A's actions and their operation data.

**Evidence.**

```ts
`this._actionsLookup = actionLookup;` ... `private _requestActionHandler(systemName: string): IAction | null { const action = this._actionsLookup[systemName];` ... the per-engine destroy closure never resets _actionsLookup
```

**Fix.** Minimal fix: in the per-engine destroy(), clear the shared lookup only if this engine still owns it: `if (this._actionsLookup === actionLookup) this._actionsLookup = {};`. That stops a destroyed engine's actions from being returned. To keep a live engine's actions reachable, merge lookups instead of replacing them. Keep a Map from engine to lookup, and have _requestActionHandler search the live lookups, newest first. Each engine's destroy() removes its own entry. This still cannot pick the requesting engine for duplicate names, because requests carry no engine identity. Real isolation means one eventbus per engine, so document that each concurrently live engine needs its own EngineFactory (and eventbus). Do not use per-engine 'request-action' responders on the shared bus: Eventbus.request returns responders[0], so the first engine would always answer.

Original suggestion: Keep the lookup per engine: register a per-engine 'request-action' responder in createEngine (removed by the engine's destroy), or key the lookups by engine and clear the engine's entry in its destroy() closure.

**Verifier.** I traced the scenario through the code and it holds. EngineFactory registers a single 'request-action' responder in its constructor (src/engine-factory.ts:90-94). That responder reads this._actionsLookup (line 138). Each createEngine() replaces the lookup with its own (line 197). The per-engine destroy() (lines 255-279) runs engineRemovers but never resets _actionsLookup. Only factory.destroy() clears it (line 110), which the existing unit test 'should clear _actionsLookup on factory destroy' covers. So after createEngine(configA) and then createEngine(configB), a requestAction operation in A (src/operation/request-action.ts:33) gets null and logs 'Unknown action'. The same happens to a DOMEventListenerController attached by A's init actions after B was created (dom-event-listener-controller.ts:64), and to a same-named action, which resolves to B's. After B's destroy(), B's actions are still returned. Running two live engines on one factory is a real tested path: the 'given two live engines...' integration test in src/test/integration/engine-factory-lifecycle.spec.ts:516.  Mitigating context lowers the severity and invalidates the proposed fix: (1) Every engine created by one factory shares one eventbus. Every other engine-scoped responder is just as unisolated. EngineEventbusAdapter registers 'request-engine-root', 'timeline-container-request', 'request-current-timeline-position' and others with onRequest, and Eventbus.request returns responders[0] (eventbus.ts:231), so the first engine wins. 'timeline-play-request' and similar broadcasts drive both engines. Two engines on one factory are already not isolated: engine B's selectElement resolves against engine A's root. This lookup problem is one symptom of that shared-eventbus design. The only thing the two-engine test checks is the global-data refcount. (2) The suggested fix (one 'request-action' responder per createEngine) does not work. Request carries no engine identity and the first responder wins, so engine A's lookup would always answer and B's own requests would fail instead.  So it is real and reachable from valid configurations, but only when one factory hosts several engines, a setup that is broken in bigger ways already. Severity: low.

**Repro test.** `given a factory that created engine A (event/timeline action 'ShowMenu') and then engine B (no 'ShowMenu'), when eventbus.request('request-action', 'ShowMenu') is called while both are live, then it returns A's ShowMenu action instead of null; and given B is then destroyed, when B's action name is requested, then it returns null`

### 35. [low] reparentElement passes its selector to jQuery(): HTML creates nodes, escapes root

[reparent-element.ts:34](src/operation/reparent-element.ts#L34) · area: lens-security-robustness · reported severity: medium · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** `appendTo(string)` runs `jQuery(string)`. A value that looks like HTML is parsed (with keepScripts) into new detached nodes, and the selected element is moved into them, so it disappears from the page. Inline handlers in the markup run. A real selector is resolved against the whole document rather than the engine root that selectElement uses. If it matches several elements, jQuery inserts clones without data or handlers into all but the last match.

**Failure scenario.** reparentElement {newParentSelector:'<div class="made"></div>'}: the probe confirmed the element is no longer in the document and its parent is the new detached div. With '<img src=x onerror=alert(1)>' the handler runs. With '.panel' on a host page that also uses .panel outside the engine root, the element moves outside the engine root (or is cloned).

**Evidence.**

```ts
const {selectedElement, newParentSelector} = operationData;
  removeProperties(operationData, 'newParentSelector');
  // appendTo moves the existing node; .remove() would strip its jQuery data and handlers
  selectedElement.appendTo(newParentSelector);
```

**Fix.** Resolve the target first: `const target = $(newParentSelector);` (ideally scoped to the engine root via `this.eventbus.request('request-engine-root')`, matching selectElement). If `target.length === 0`, throw `reparentElement: '${newParentSelector}' matched no element`. If `target.length > 1`, throw an ambiguity error, or document and use `target.first()`. Then call `selectedElement.appendTo(target.first())` so jQuery never clones.

Original suggestion: Resolve the target through `request-engine-root` with `root.find(newParentSelector)`, as selectElement does. Throw, naming the operation, when the match count is not exactly 1, and never pass the raw string to `jQuery()`/`appendTo()`.

**Verifier.** src/operation/reparent-element.ts:34 calls `selectedElement.appendTo(newParentSelector)` with no check on the target. Nothing upstream guards it: the selector is not resolved, counted or scoped to the engine root, and `selectElement`'s warning applies only to `selectedElement`, not to the new parent. jQuery's appendTo inserts `this.clone(true)` into every target except the last. clone(true) copies user data with a shallow `jQuery.extend({}, old)`, so array values are shared by reference. I ran a temporary vitest probe against the real operation and real jQuery in jsdom (the probe is deleted now): with three `.slot` targets I got 3 copies, and `copies.eq(0).data('eligiusEngineControllers') === copies.eq(2).data('eligiusEngineControllers')` was true. Splicing the array on copy 0 emptied it on copy 2. With `newParentSelector: '.nope'` the element stayed under its old parent and nothing was logged or thrown. The operation's TypeDoc says it "moves the given selected element to the new parent" (singular), so cloning is not documented intent. The existing spec (src/test/unit/operation/reparent-element.spec.ts) covers only a single-match target and a mock; it has no case for multiple matches or no match. Severity stays low: it needs an ambiguous or misspelled selector in the configuration. A correct configuration never triggers it.

**Repro test.** `given an element with controller data and three .slot elements, when reparentElement is applied with newParentSelector '.slot', then the element exists exactly once in the DOM (fails today: 3 copies sharing one controllers array)`

### 36. [low] LocaleLoader cycle detection and cache bypassed by unnormalised ./ and ../ refs

[locale-loader.ts:251](src/locale/locale-loader.ts#L251) · area: eventbus-locale-util · reported severity: medium · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** Nested $ref URLs are joined by string concatenation and never normalised. visitedPaths and the cache compare the raw strings, so the same file reached through a different spelling ('locales/b/../a/x.json' vs 'locales/a/x.json') counts as a new URL. A cycle written with relative segments is never detected. Each round adds more segments, so the loader fetches without end and the promise never settles.

**Failure scenario.** locales/en/a.json contains {x: {$ref: '../shared/b.json'}} and locales/shared/b.json contains {y: {$ref: '../en/a.json'}}. Resolution visits 'locales/en/../shared/b.json', then 'locales/en/../shared/../en/a.json', then 'locales/en/../shared/../en/../shared/b.json', and so on. Every URL is new, so no 'Circular reference' error is thrown. Fetches never stop, and EngineFactory's locale promise and engine.init() hang. The simplest form, a file that refs './self.json', does the same. A file reached through two spellings is also fetched twice instead of coming from the cache.

**Evidence.**

```ts
const parentDir = parentUrl.substring(0, lastSlash + 1);
    return `${parentDir}${ref}`;
...
        if (visitedPaths.has(nestedUrl)) {
          throw new Error(
            `[LocaleLoader] Circular reference detected: ...
```

**Fix.** Normalise every resolved URL before it is used as a key in visitedPaths, _cache and _loading, and pass the normalised URL to fetch. Two cases: - Absolute or protocol-relative URLs, when a base is available: use `new URL(ref, new URL(parentUrl, globalThis.document?.baseURI ?? 'http://localhost/')).href`. - Relative strings: use a small segment normaliser instead of always going through `new URL()`. That keeps relative URLs relative, which the existing `toHaveBeenCalledWith('./en-US.json')` tests depend on. The normaliser splits off any `?query` or `#hash`, drops '.' segments, pops the previous segment on '..' (keeping a leading '..' when nothing is left to pop), and rejoins.  Apply the normaliser in `_resolveUrl` and `_resolveRelativeToParent`. Add tests for a '../' A-to-B-to-A cycle, a './self.json' self-reference, and one file reached through two spellings that must be fetched only once.

Original suggestion: Normalise every URL before using it as a key. In _resolveUrl and _resolveRelativeToParent use new URL(ref, new URL(parentUrl, document.baseURI ?? 'http://localhost/')).href, or a small posix-style normaliser that collapses '.' and '..' segments, and use the result for visitedPaths, _cache and _loading. Add a test for a '../' cycle and a './self.json' ref.

**Verifier.** I traced the scenario through src/locale/locale-loader.ts and it holds. Nothing normalises a URL anywhere. `_resolveRelativeToParent` (lines 251-269) returns `parentDir + ref`. `_resolveUrl` (168-187) only adds baseUrl. `visitedPaths` (lines 76 and 205), `_cache` and `_loading` (116-138) all use the raw string as the key.  Trace with no baseUrl (EngineFactory line 313 creates `new LocaleLoader()` with no options): 1. Load 'locales/en/a.json'; visited = {that string}. 2. Its `../shared/b.json` ref resolves to 'locales/en/../shared/b.json'. That string is new, so it is fetched (the browser's URL parser collapses the dot segments, so the real file comes back). 3. b's `../en/a.json` ref resolves to 'locales/en/../shared/../en/a.json'. This differs from 'locales/en/a.json', so no cycle error is thrown and a is fetched again. 4. Each round adds segments, so the check never matches and every fetch misses the cache.  A `./self.json` ref behaves the same way: 'locales/en/./self.json', then 'locales/en/././self.json', and so on.  The async recursion does not overflow the stack. It only ends when the browser rejects an oversized URL or memory runs out. Until then, EngineFactory's `ready` promise and `engine.init()` stay pending while requests keep firing.  Nothing guards against this: - No normaliser exists anywhere in the call chain. - The only cycle test (locale-loader.spec.ts:155) uses absolute '/locales/a.json' paths. Its comment says "Use absolute paths to avoid relative path resolution issues", so the gap is known and untested. - Tests for `./` refs only cover single-level loads.  Severity is lower than claimed. A valid configuration never has a cyclic locale graph. Cycle detection exists to catch an authoring mistake, and the bug is that the mistake causes a hang and endless fetches instead of the documented 'Circular reference' error. That shows up right away in development. The other effect, the same file fetched twice under two spellings, only costs a redundant request. So this is low.

**Repro test.** `given locales/en/a.json refs '../shared/b.json' and locales/shared/b.json refs '../en/a.json', when load() is called, then it rejects with a circular reference error instead of fetching without end`

### 37. [low] LocaleManager.t() returns objects or functions' results for non-leaf keys

[locale-manager.ts:152](src/locale/locale-manager.ts#L152) · area: eventbus-locale-util · reported severity: medium · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** rosetta 1.1.0's t() returns the raw dlv() value when it is neither a string nor a function. The '?? ''' guard only catches null and undefined, so a key that names a namespace returns the nested object, typed as string. dlv also walks inherited properties: 'constructor' resolves to Object, which rosetta calls as a function translation.

**Failure scenario.** locales {'en-US': {nav: {home: 'Home'}}} and a LabelController with translationKey 'nav'. request-translation returns {home: 'Home'}. sanitizeHtml(obj) finds no '<' in '[object Object]' and returns the object unchanged, and selectedElement.html(obj) renders nothing. With attributeName 'href', isSafeUrl(obj) calls obj.replace and throws a TypeError in attach(). translationKey 'constructor' returns the params object (or {}) instead of ''. A function translation that returns a number also passes through as a non-string.

**Evidence.**

```ts
const result = this._rosetta.t(key, params, targetLocale) ?? '';
// rosetta: var val = dlv(tree[lang || locale], key, '');
//  if (typeof val === 'function') return val(params);
//  if (typeof val === 'string') return tmpl(val, params);
//  return val;
```

**Fix.** In LocaleManager.t(), resolve the key path yourself over own properties first. Use Object.hasOwn on each segment, accepting both a string key with dots and an array key. If the path does not exist as own properties, treat it as missing: return '' and warn in debug mode. Then call rosetta. If typeof result !== 'string', return '' and, in debug mode, warn that the key names a namespace or a function that returned something other than a string. This keeps the return type honest and also fixes the missing debug warning for these keys. Optionally, give isSafeUrl/sanitizeHtml a cheap typeof guard as defence in depth.

Original suggestion: After calling rosetta, return result only when typeof result === 'string'. Otherwise return '' and, in debug mode, warn that the key resolves to a non-string (namespace or function result). Look keys up only through own properties, for example by resolving the path yourself with Object.hasOwn before delegating to rosetta, so prototype members never resolve.

**Verifier.** I traced this through the installed code and it holds. The node_modules/rosetta/dist/index.mjs file is version 1.1.0. Its t() calls dlv(tree[lang||locale], key, ''). It calls the value if it is a function, interpolates it if it is a string, and otherwise returns it unchanged. The installed dlv does `t = t ? t[e[n]] : r`, which is a plain property access, so inherited members like 'constructor' resolve too. tree[lang] is an ordinary object, because mergeLocaleData spreads into `{}` and rosetta's set() calls Object.assign. So 'constructor' resolves to Object, and rosetta calls it as Object(params): you get back the params object, or {} when there are no params.  In LocaleManager.t() (src/locale/locale-manager.ts:152), `?? ''` only catches null and undefined, and the return is typed string without any check. Nothing filters the value on the way out. LocaleEventbusAdapter's 'request-translation' handler (src/adapters/locale-eventbus-adapter.ts:136-139) returns it as is, and LabelController._setLabel's `?? ''` doesn't catch it either.  Two failures follow: - **No attributeName:** sanitizeHtml(obj) runs `/[<&]/.test(obj)` on '[object Object]', finds no match and returns the object. jQuery .html(obj) then falls through to empty().append(obj), and append of a non-node plain object adds nothing. The label goes blank, and no "Missing translation" debug warning fires, because the result is not ''. - **URL attribute such as href:** isSafeUrl(obj) calls normalizeUrl, which calls url.replace, so attach() throws a TypeError.  The debug-mode `_checkMissingInterpolationVars` check returns early for non-strings, so it guards nothing here. No test in src/test/unit/locale covers a namespace key or a prototype key.  **Severity is lower than claimed.** This only happens with a misconfigured translationKey: one that names a namespace instead of a leaf, or the literal 'constructor'/'toString'. Function translations, and function results that are not strings, cannot come from JSON configuration; they are only reachable through programmatic loadLocale. In the common case the result is a blank label, much like a missing key, but without the warning. The throw only happens for URL attributes, and errors thrown from attach now go through the engine error path. So: low.

**Repro test.** `given locale en-US with {nav: {home: 'Home'}}, when t('nav') or t('constructor') is called, then it returns an empty string`

### 38. [low] Scroll loop reset smooth-scrolls back to the top, replaying every action backwards

[scroll-position-source.ts:200](src/timelineproviders/position-sources/scroll-position-source.ts#L200) · area: timeline-providers-adapters · reported severity: medium · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** On reaching the end with loop = true, the source scrolls back to the top with behavior 'smooth' while it is still active. Each scroll event during the rewind is a backward position update, and the engine reconciles each one by starting the actions that cover that position and ending them again. A loop restart therefore runs the start and end operations of every action in the timeline in reverse order instead of jumping to 0.

**Failure scenario.** Looping scroll timeline (duration 60) with actions [10,20] and [30,40]. The user scrolls to the bottom and 'end' is emitted. The smooth scroll emits positions such as 45, 35, 25, 15, 5, 0. The engine starts and ends [30,40], then starts and ends [10,20], and only then sees 'start'.

**Evidence.**

```ts
if (this.loop) {
        // Reset to start
        this._element.scrollTo({
          top: 0,
          behavior: 'smooth',
        });
      }
```

**Fix.** Change the loop branch in _handleScroll to jump to the top and report the restart at once:  ```ts if (this.loop) {   this._element.scrollTo({top: 0, behavior: 'instant'});   this._lastPosition = 0;   this._wasAtStart = true;   this.setPosition(0);   this.emitBoundary('start');   return; } ```  1. **Use 'instant', not 'auto'.** 'auto' still follows a CSS `scroll-behavior: smooth` set on the container, so the scroll could still animate. 2. **Return after the reset.** This skips the start-boundary check and the `_wasAtStart = position === 0` line below it, which would otherwise set `_wasAtStart` back to false. 3. **No ignore flag is needed for the late scroll event.** The instant scroll still fires one scroll event at scrollTop 0. When it arrives, previousPosition is already 0 and `_wasAtStart` is true, so 'start' is not emitted twice. setPosition(0) changes nothing. The engine's `_lastPosition === position` check drops the duplicate. 4. **Test.** Add a spec whose scrollTo mock fires several scroll events with decreasing scrollTop values. It should assert that no position other than 0 is emitted after 'end', and that scrollTo is called with `behavior: 'instant'`.

Original suggestion: Reset with `behavior: 'instant'`. Set _lastPosition and the position to 0 and emit 'start' directly, ignoring scroll events until the reset scroll has been applied. Add a test asserting that no intermediate positions are emitted during a loop reset.

**Verifier.** I traced the scenario through the real code, and it holds. I looked for a guard or an intended design that would make it harmless and found none.  1. **The reset is a smooth scroll while the source is still active.** In scroll-position-source.ts, _handleScroll (lines 195-204) emits 'end' and then calls `this._element.scrollTo({top: 0, behavior: 'smooth'})`. The state stays 'active' and the scroll listener stays attached. Nothing suppresses the scroll events that follow, and nothing resets _lastPosition or the position. In a browser, a smooth scroll fires a scroll event on every frame, and each one runs _handleScroll. That calls setPosition() with an intermediate, decreasing position.  2. **The engine does not filter these positions.** In eligius-engine.ts, the onBoundaryReached 'end' handler (lines 629-637) only emits timelineComplete. It does not pause the engine or block position updates. The onPosition callback calls _onTimeHandler (line 1053). That only ignores updates when _seekDepth > 0, which is true only inside engine.seek() and not during a source-initiated scroll. So every intermediate position reaches _dispatchActionsBetween.  3. **Each backward step replays actions.** For a backward step, _dispatchActionsBetween (the to < from branch, about lines 1115-1137) restarts an inactive action once the position falls back inside its range, and ends an active action once the position falls below its start. With actions [10,20] and [30,40] and positions 60, 45, 35, 25, 15, 5, 0:    - 45 to 35 starts [30,40], because its end key 40 lies in the range and its start 30 is at or below 35.    - 35 to 25 ends it again.    - The same happens to [10,20] between 25 and 5.    - 'start' and timelineRestart arrive only after all of that.    An instant jump from 60 to 0 restarts nothing: the start 30 is not at or below 0, and the pending start is dropped. That is the outcome the loop should have.  4. **It contradicts the documented intent.** The earlier expansion proposal in specs/main/codebase-audit-report.md (around line 3127) states: "Loop wrap-around (boundary 'start' resets to 0) must be treated as a seek, not as a backward scrub." The comment "Reset to start" also points to a jump.  5. **No test covers it.** The one loop test in src/test/unit/timelineproviders/position-sources/scroll-position-source.spec.ts (about line 690) mocks scrollTo to set scrollTop to 0 at once and fire a single scroll event. It never models the intermediate events of a smooth scroll, so it cannot catch this.  **Possible defences, and why they fail.** - A user scrolling back up by hand gets the same replay, and the engine supports that on purpose. But the loop reset is not user input. The source starts it itself, right after reporting completion, so restarting a loop runs every action's start and end operations in reverse. Those operations include broadcasts, requests and DOM changes. - seek() also smooth-scrolls, but that is a separate path and does not excuse the loop reset.  **Severity: low, not medium.** It needs loop: true on a scroll timeline, which is a valid configuration but unusual. While it happens, the replayed state matches the content scrolling past on screen, so the visual effect is mostly redundant work and repeated side effects rather than a corrupted state. After the rewind the engine ends up consistent at 0.

**Repro test.** `given loop enabled and a smooth scrollTo that fires intermediate scroll events, when the scroll reaches the end, then no position between duration and 0 is emitted and scrollTo is called with behavior instant`

### 39. [low] 'timeline-switch' vertical mode is dead: nothing consumes onTimelineChangeRequest

[keyboard-step-source.ts:284](src/timelineproviders/position-sources/keyboard-step-source.ts#L284) · area: timeline-providers-adapters · reported severity: medium · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** With verticalMode 'timeline-switch', Up and Down are bound, their default (page scroll) is prevented, and _emitTimelineChange calls the callbacks registered with onTimelineChangeRequest. Neither EligiusEngine nor EngineFactory registers one; grep finds no consumer outside the class and its tests. A configuration using `{systemName: 'KeyboardStepSource', verticalMode: 'timeline-switch'}` swallows Up and Down and does nothing. onMarkerJump also has no consumer, so chapter jumps are not visible on the eventbus.

**Failure scenario.** An engine is configured with a KeyboardStepSource timeline (verticalMode: 'timeline-switch') and two timelines. Pressing Down calls event.preventDefault() and emits 'next' to zero callbacks. The engine stays on the same timeline and the page does not scroll either.

**Evidence.**

```ts
if (this._verticalMode !== 'disabled') {
      hotkeys(KEY_BINDINGS.verticalPrev, options, event => {
        event.preventDefault();
        this._handleVerticalPrev();
      });
```

**Fix.** In EligiusEngine._prepareSource, duck-type the source. If it has an onTimelineChangeRequest function, register a callback that finds the index of _currentTimelineUri among the configured timelines, steps it to the previous or next timeline (wrapping or clamping to match the source's wrapNavigation), and calls this.switchTimeline(uri, 0). Report failures through the existing error event, not as an unhandled rejection. Also forward the request to the eventbus (for example a new 'timeline-change-request' event), and forward onMarkerJump as an event too, so configurations can react to both. Add an engine-level integration test. As a smaller interim step, skip binding Up and Down in timeline-switch mode until a callback is registered, so the page scroll is not blocked when nothing listens.

Original suggestion: Wire it in the engine: in _prepareSource, when the source has onTimelineChangeRequest, register a callback that switches to the previous or next timeline in configuration order. Alternatively, have the source broadcast a request event through an injected eventbus. Until then, do not bind or preventDefault Up and Down when there is no listener.

**Verifier.** I could not refute the core claim. The report overstates the impact, though.  Reachable from a valid configuration: EligiusResourceImporter imports everything from @timelineproviders/index.ts, which exports KeyboardStepSource (line 25). EngineFactory.createPositionSourceForTimeline (src/engine-factory.ts:356-371) passes `{...options, duration}` to the constructor, so `{systemName: 'KeyboardStepSource', verticalMode: 'timeline-switch'}` arrives intact. The generated schema (raf-timeline-provider-settings.json) advertises the 'timeline-switch' enum value.  No consumer: a grep of src/ for onTimelineChangeRequest and onMarkerJump finds only keyboard-step-source.ts and its spec. EligiusEngine._prepareSource (eligius-engine.ts:616-645) registers only onPosition, onBoundaryReached and onActivated. EngineFactory, the adapters and the eventbus never register a timeline-change callback. When verticalMode is not 'disabled', _bindKeys (line 284) binds Up and Down with event.preventDefault(). _handleVerticalNext then calls _emitTimelineChange('next'), which loops over an empty _timelineChangeCallbacks array. With JSON-only configuration, Up and Down are swallowed (no page scroll) and the timeline never changes. The unit tests (keyboard-step-source.spec.ts:626-680) only check that a manually registered callback fires. No engine or integration test checks that a timeline switch happens.  Why I lowered the severity: - **The documented contract is met.** CHANGELOG 2.1.0 and the schema say the mode "emits timeline change requests", and the source does emit them to its callback list. Nothing in the docs promises that the engine switches timelines. - **A host has a hook.** IEligiusEngine.init() returns the active IPositionSource (types.ts:202), so host code can call `(await engine.init()).onTimelineChangeRequest(d => engine.switchTimeline(...))`. - **The hook is incomplete.** It only reaches the first timeline's source. Every other timeline gets its own source from createPositionSourceForTimeline, and the engine never exposes those, so the host cannot keep switching after the first switch. - **Chapter jumps still work.** In chapter-navigation mode the jump itself goes through setPosition, so it reaches the engine. Only the extra onMarkerJump notification has no listener. - **Limited harm.** No crash and no state corruption. The worst case is a feature that does nothing out of the box while it blocks arrow-key scrolling, and it needs an opt-in keyboard source. That is low, not medium.

**Repro test.** `given an engine created by EngineFactory with two timelines whose positionSource is {systemName: 'KeyboardStepSource', verticalMode: 'timeline-switch'}, when the engine is initialised and started and the Down arrow is pressed, then engine.currentTimelineUri is the second timeline's uri`

### 40. [low] Resolver accepts any function export, including classes, as an operation

[configuration-resolver.ts:211](src/configuration/configuration-resolver.ts#L211) · area: configuration-tools · reported severity: medium · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** resolveOperation only checks that the imported value is a function. EligiusResourceImporter also serves controllers, position sources, eventbus classes and EligiusEngine, all of which have typeof 'function'. A systemName naming one of them passes fail-fast validation, and the action then throws a TypeError at runtime. The importer's getOperationNames() is used only for the 'did you mean' hint, not for validation.

**Failure scenario.** A config with startOperations: [{id:'1', systemName:'LabelController'}], where the user confused the controller name with addControllerToElement, resolves without error. When the action starts, operationInfo.instance.call(...) throws 'Class constructor LabelController cannot be invoked without new', with no JSON path.

**Evidence.**

```ts
instance = importer.import(systemName)?.[systemName];
...
  if (!isFunction(instance)) {
    throw new Error(createUnknownOperationMessage(importer, systemName, path));
  }
```

**Fix.** In resolveOperation, after the import, check the names: `const known = getKnownOperationNames(importer); if (known.length > 0 && !known.includes(systemName)) throw new Error(createUnknownOperationMessage(importer, systemName, path));`. Keep the isFunction check as the fallback for importers that do not expose getOperationNames. With the default importer, the 'did you mean' hint for 'LabelController' will then suggest operation names such as addControllerToElement. Add a resolver spec that uses a controller name as an operation systemName.

Original suggestion: When the importer exposes getOperationNames(), require `getKnownOperationNames(importer).includes(systemName)` and throw createUnknownOperationMessage otherwise. Fall back to the isFunction check only for importers without that method. Add a resolver spec for a controller name used as an operation.

**Verifier.** I traced the scenario through the real code and it holds. In resolveOperation (src/configuration/configuration-resolver.ts:194-221), the only check is isFunction(instance). EligiusResourceImporter.import (src/importer/eligius-resource-importer.ts) looks names up in this order: operations, controllers, providers, eventbus exports, then EligiusEngine. So import('LabelController') returns {LabelController: class}. typeof a class is 'function', so the check passes and the resolver returns an IResolvedOperation whose instance is the class. getKnownOperationNames (getOperationNames) is only used inside createUnknownOperationMessage to build the hint. It is never used to validate. When the action runs, Action.executeOperation calls instance.call(...), which throws 'Class constructor LabelController cannot be invoked without new'. The catch block wraps it with withOperationContext, so the error does name operationSystemName 'LabelController' and its index, but not the JSON path. That breaks the fail-fast promise in CLAUDE.md. No resolver spec covers a non-operation name: the specs at lines 382/482 only test misspelled names that the importer rejects. Mitigation that lowers the severity: the generated JSON schema (jsonschema/eligius-configuration.json, operation configuration oneOf over operations/*.json) rejects systemName 'LabelController'. So this only happens with a configuration that is already schema-invalid, because the engine does not run schema validation itself. The runtime failure is also caught and reported through the error path with the operation name. It is neither silent nor corrupting. Severity: low, not medium.

**Repro test.** `given a timeline action whose startOperations contains systemName 'LabelController', when ConfigurationResolver.process is called with EligiusResourceImporter, then it throws "Unknown operation 'LabelController' at timelines[0].timelineActions[0].startOperations[0].systemName"`

### 41. [low] Schema requires props that lack @required, e.g. setElementContent.insertionType

[generate-schemas.ts:331](src/tools/schema-tools/generate-schemas.ts#L331) · area: configuration-tools · reported severity: medium · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** isRequired also uses `dataSchema.required`, which ts-json-schema-generator fills with every non-optional TS property. The schema therefore requires properties that the metadata (driven by @required) and the runtime treat as optional. The schema and the metadata diverge.

**Failure scenario.** {systemName:'setElementContent', operationData:{template:'<p/>'}} works at runtime (insertionType defaults to 'overwrite') and its metadata marks neither property required. It fails the schema because operationData.required is ['template','insertionType']. startAction without actionOperationData (defaults to {} since this release) is likewise rejected.

**Evidence.**

```ts
const isRequired =
      propSchema.description?.includes('@required') ||
      propSchema.required === true ||
      dataSchema.required?.includes(propName);
--- set-element-content.ts
  const {
    insertionType = 'overwrite',
```

**Fix.** Fix the source types first. Mark properties the operation defaults as optional: - `insertionType?: ...` in ISetElementContentOperationData - `actionOperationData?: TOperationData` in IStartActionOperationData  Then audit the other operation interfaces for destructuring defaults. That keeps the TS type, the schema and the runtime consistent without changing the generator.  Then make the generator and metadata-generator use the same rule. Either: - derive `required` only from `@required` in both (drop `dataSchema.required?.includes(propName)`), or - keep the TS-required clause and have metadata-generator also treat non-optional, non-dependency properties as required.  Add a generate-schemas spec that loads each operation's metadata and asserts that the schema's operationData.required equals the set of metadata properties with `required: true`.

Original suggestion: Derive required only from the @required tag, matching metadata-generator. Alternatively, make the TS interfaces match the tags (insertionType?: ..., actionOperationData?: ...). Add a test asserting that schema `required` equals metadata `required: true` for every operation.

**Verifier.** I traced it through the real code and the generated output, and the claim holds.  1. In src/tools/schema-tools/generate-schemas.ts lines 331-334, a property counts as required when `dataSchema.required?.includes(propName)` is true. ts-json-schema-generator fills that list with every non-optional TS property. `@required` is not the only source.  2. In src/operation/set-element-content.ts, the interface declares `insertionType: 'overwrite' | 'append' | 'prepend'` with no `?` and no `@required` tag. The operation defaults it at line 41 (`insertionType = 'overwrite'`). `template` also has no `@required` tag.  3. The generated jsonschema/operations/set-element-content.json has top-level required ['systemName','operationData'] and operationData.required ['template','insertionType']. So `{systemName:'setElementContent', operationData:{template:'<p/>'}}` fails the schema even though the runtime accepts it.  4. The generated src/operation/metadata/set-element-content.ts marks neither property `required`. So the schema and the metadata really do disagree.  5. startAction has the same problem. `actionOperationData: TOperationData` is non-optional in TS, while the runtime defaults it with `actionOperationData = {}`. The generated start-action.json requires both operationData and operationData.actionOperationData.  6. Tests: src/test/unit/tools/schema-tools/generate-schemas.spec.ts only asserts required lists where `@required` and the TS type agree (addClass className, storageType, properties, dependency-only ops). Nothing checks the schema against the metadata or against runtime defaults.  7. The behaviour is not intentional. Commit b44ce00f reworked this function so that operationData "is only required when one of its properties is", but it kept the TS-required clause.  Why it is low, not medium: - Nothing in the runtime validates against this schema: no ajv, and no use of the schema outside src/tools. - ConfigurationResolver and the engine accept the config and run it correctly. - The impact is limited to external consumers of the published schema (IDE validation, the Eligian DSL). They get a false error and are pushed into adding a redundant `insertionType`. - The schema does faithfully mirror the declared TS type, which also (wrongly) makes the property mandatory. So the root cause is arguably the interfaces being wrong as much as the generator.  Nothing breaks at runtime; the problem is only false negatives in schema-based tooling.

**Repro test.** `given the generated setElementContent schema, when reading operationData.required, then insertionType is not listed because the operation defaults it to overwrite and its metadata does not mark it required`

### 42. [low] extendController lets a nested __proto__ key replace the controller's prototype

[extend-controller.ts:33](src/operation/extend-controller.ts#L33) · area: operations-a · reported severity: low · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** The `__proto__` fix in this round rejects only a top-level `__proto__` key in configured operation data. `controllerExtension` is a nested object, and deepCopy keeps an own `__proto__` key as a data property. `Object.assign(controllerInstance, controllerExtension)` then sets `__proto__` through the setter and swaps the controller's prototype. A probe confirmed it: with extension `{"__proto__":{"x":1}}`, afterwards `typeof controllerInstance.init === 'undefined'` and `instanceof` the controller class is false.

**Failure scenario.** JSON config: getControllerInstance {systemName:'LabelController'}, then extendController {controllerExtension: {"__proto__": {}}}, then addControllerToElement. The run throws 'controllerInstance.init is not a function', or a crafted object installs arbitrary inherited members on the controller.

**Evidence.**

```ts
operationData.controllerInstance = Object.assign(
    controllerInstance,
    controllerExtension
  );
```

**Fix.** In extendController, check before assigning: if (Object.hasOwn(controllerExtension, '__proto__')) throw new Error("extendController: controllerExtension must not contain a '__proto__' key"). Optionally, instead of Object.assign, copy the keys with Object.defineProperty in a loop over Object.keys, skipping any key where isForbiddenPropertyChainSegment(key) is true (from '@operation/helper/assert-safe-property-chain.ts'). A more general fix would make copyConfiguredOperationData reject a forbidden key at any nesting depth.

Original suggestion: Copy the extension with `Object.entries(...)` and `Object.defineProperty` (or skip forbidden keys via `isForbiddenPropertyChainSegment`). Throw `extendController: controllerExtension must not contain '__proto__'`.

**Verifier.** I traced the path and confirmed it with a test. The test file is deleted again.  1. ConfigurationResolver (src/configuration/configuration-resolver.ts:218) runs deepCopy on operationData. 2. deepCopy (src/operation/helper/deep-copy.ts:51-61) writes each key with Object.defineProperty. A nested own "__proto__" key from JSON.parse therefore stays an own, enumerable data property. That is deliberate. 3. At run time, copyConfiguredOperationData (src/action/action.ts:260-285) rejects only a TOP-LEVEL own __proto__ (Object.hasOwn(operationData, '__proto__')). It does not look inside nested objects, so {controllerExtension: {"__proto__": {...}}} gets through. It is deep-copied again, which keeps the key, and merged into the running operation data. 4. extendController (src/operation/extend-controller.ts:33) calls Object.assign(controllerInstance, controllerExtension). Object.assign writes each property with [[Set]]. The controller instance inherits the Object.prototype __proto__ accessor, so the setter runs and replaces the controller's prototype.  No guard sits elsewhere on this path. Neither resolvePropertyValues nor the action loop filters nested keys. The two existing tests in src/test/unit/operation/extend-controller.spec.ts cover only ordinary keys.  My test did this: deepCopy(JSON.parse('{"controllerExtension":{"__proto__":{"x":1}}}')), merged it with {controllerInstance: new LabelController()}, then called applyOperation(extendController, ...). Afterwards controllerInstance instanceof LabelController === false, typeof init === 'undefined' and controllerInstance.x === 1. All three assertions passed.  Why low severity: - The damage stays on that one controller instance. It does not reach Object.prototype globally. - Only the configuration author can trigger it, and they control the config anyway. - JSON cannot express function-valued extensions, so this operation is used mainly through the TS fluent API. - The project's own security rule asks for a guard here, and the top-level-only check is an incomplete fix. That is the real defect.

**Repro test.** `given a controllerExtension parsed from JSON with an own __proto__ key, when extendController runs on a LabelController instance, then it throws naming extendController and the controller keeps its LabelController prototype`

### 43. [low] RoutingController writes position -1 into the URL and replays it as a start position

[routing-controller.ts:139](src/controllers/routing-controller.ts#L139) · area: controllers · reported severity: low · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** _pushState asks 'request-current-video-position' through a callback, but nothing in the engine answers it, so currentPosition stays -1 and lands in history state and URL (#/id/-1). On reload or back navigation, _parsePosition accepts -1 as a finite number and broadcasts it as the requested position.

**Failure scenario.** 'push-history-state' arrives with {navigationData: nav2, title, position: -1} (or with position omitted, through the before-request path). The URL becomes #/nav2/-1. Reloading the page broadcasts request-video-url [1, -1, true], a negative start position.

**Evidence.**

```ts
let currentPosition = state.position !== undefined ? state.position : -1;
      if (currentPosition < 0) {
        ...
        this.eventbus?.broadcast('request-current-video-position', [
...
          `#/${state.navigationData.id}/${currentPosition}`
...
  private _parsePosition(value: unknown): number {
    const position = Number.parseFloat(String(value));
    return Number.isFinite(position) ? position : 0;
```

**Fix.** In _pushState, when position is undefined or negative, get the position with `const p = this.eventbus?.request<number>('request-current-timeline-position')`. Apply the existing rewind rule: `currentPosition = typeof p === 'number' && Number.isFinite(p) ? Math.max(0, p - 3) : undefined`. When currentPosition is undefined, leave the position segment out of the URL and the position out of the state, so the URL is `#/<id>`. In _parsePosition, return 0 for negative values: `return Number.isFinite(position) && position >= 0 ? position : 0;`. Remove the dead 'request-current-video-position' broadcast. Also fix the spec so it no longer mocks a responder that does not exist.

Original suggestion: Request the position with eventbus.request('request-current-timeline-position') and clamp to >= 0. In _parsePosition return 0 for negative values, and omit the position segment when it is unknown.

**Verifier.** I traced the code and the defect is real, though it is hard to reach.  1) Nothing in the engine answers 'request-current-video-position'. Searching src/ finds only the event typing, its generated metadata, the broadcast in routing-controller.ts:144 and a mocked responder in RoutingController.spec.ts:276. The engine answers only 'request-current-timeline-position' (eligius-engine.ts:819, engine-eventbus-adapter.ts:156). So the callback at line 141 never runs, and currentPosition stays -1 when it starts at -1.  2) There is one reliable way to start at -1. On 'before-request-video-url' with isHistoryRequest false, _handleBeforeRequestVideoUrl broadcasts 'request-current-navigation'. NavigationController._handleRequestCurrentNavigation (navigation-controller.ts:91-106) answers with {navigationData, title}, with no position. _pushState then sets currentPosition = -1 (line 139). Nothing replaces that value, so pushState or replaceState writes {position: -1} and the URL '#/<id>/-1' (lines 154-176).  3) The value is replayed. On reload, attach() calls _parsePosition('-1'), and on popstate it calls _parsePosition(-1). Both return -1 because -1 is finite (lines 91-94). The controller then broadcasts 'request-video-url' [idx, -1, true].  Why it is still low severity, and arguments against it: - The built-in NavigationController never produces -1. Its _handleNavigateVideoUrl defaults requestedVideoPosition to 0, so _pushCurrentState(0) sets position 0. That covers clicks and 'navigate-to-video-url' without a position argument. - Nothing in src/ broadcasts 'before-request-video-url'. Neither the engine nor any built-in component handles 'request-video-url'. The bad start position therefore reaches only host code or a configured event action. - RoutingController's own JSDoc says "This is a work in progress, do not use this yet please".  A valid configuration can still trigger it. A broadcastEvent operation with eventName 'before-request-video-url', while a NavigationController and a RoutingController are attached, puts '/-1' in the URL. A host or configuration that broadcasts 'push-history-state' with position -1 or no position does the same.  The existing test ('should handle push-history-state event') mocks a responder for 'request-current-video-position', so it hides the bug. It only checks that pushState was called, not the URL.

**Repro test.** `given a RoutingController and a responder for request-current-navigation that returns visible navigation data without a position on a real Eventbus, when before-request-video-url is broadcast with [1, 0, false], then window.history.pushState is not called with a URL ending in '/-1' and the pushed state position is not negative`

### 44. [low] RoutingController.attach pushes a duplicate history entry instead of replacing it

[routing-controller.ts:58](src/controllers/routing-controller.ts#L58) · area: controllers · reported severity: low · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** With no hash in the URL, attach calls history.pushState to add #/firstId. That leaves an extra entry for the same page, so the first Back press stays on the page (popstate with null state, which falls back to navigation[0] and re-requests it) instead of leaving.

**Failure scenario.** Open /presentation.html, attach the RoutingController, and the history becomes [presentation.html, presentation.html#/intro]. Press Back: the URL loses the hash, popstate (state null) re-requests the intro, and the user needs a second Back to leave the page.

**Evidence.**

```ts
} else if (this.navigation?.length) {
      window.history.pushState(
        {navigationId: this.navigation[0].id},
        '',
        `#/${this.navigation[0].id}`
      );
```

**Fix.** In attach (line 58), use `window.history.replaceState({navigationId: this.navigation[0].id}, '', '#/' + this.navigation[0].id)` so the initial entry gets the state without adding a new entry. Update the spec at RoutingController.spec.ts:228 to expect replaceState and to check that pushState is not called. This also means a later popstate back to the start entry carries a real navigationId instead of null.

Original suggestion: Use history.replaceState for the initial state, and update the test that expects pushState.

**Verifier.** I traced this through src/controllers/routing-controller.ts. When there is no hash, `_getQueryVariable(0)` returns '' (lines 120-131). If navigation is non-empty, attach calls `window.history.pushState({navigationId: nav[0].id}, '', '#/' + id)` (lines 57-63). That adds a new history entry on top of the original one, which has null state. Pressing Back is a same-document, hash-only navigation, so popstate fires with `event.state === null`. `_handlePopstate` calls `_findNavigation(undefined)`. Because the id is not a string, it falls back to `this.navigation?.[0]` (lines 84-89). So the handler broadcasts `highlight-navigation` and `request-video-url` for the first entry at position 0 (lines 72-77). The user is still on the page and needs a second Back to leave, so the reported scenario holds.  I found nothing that prevents it. No guard checks `history.state` or uses replaceState for the initial entry. Elsewhere, `_pushState` deliberately uses replaceState when the navigation id is unchanged (lines 159-170), which shows the author's intent is to avoid duplicate entries for the same navigation.  The only test, RoutingController.spec.ts:228 ('should push initial state when no navigation ID in URL'), asserts pushState with a mocked history. It documents the current behaviour but does not check what happens on Back, so it does not show the extra entry is intended.  Severity stays low. The class JSDoc says "This is a work in progress, do not use this yet please". The effect is an extra Back press plus a reload of the first video, with no data loss and no engine state corruption.

**Repro test.** `given a URL without a hash and non-empty navigation data, when RoutingController.attach is called, then history.replaceState is called with {navigationId: 'nav1'} and '#/nav1' and history.pushState is not called`

### 45. [low] NavigationController autoNext does not update active navigation point or highlight

[navigation-controller.ts:216](src/controllers/navigation-controller.ts#L216) · area: controllers · reported severity: low · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** On 'video-complete' with autoNext, the controller broadcasts request-video-url for the next item directly. It skips _handleNavigateVideoUrl, so activeNavigationPoint, the menu highlight and the pushed history state all keep pointing at the finished item.

**Failure scenario.** Item 0 has autoNext and next = item 1. 'video-complete' [0] fires and item 1 starts, but the menu still highlights nav_0. 'request-current-navigation' returns item 0, so RoutingController writes #/item0 into history for content that is item 1.

**Evidence.**

```ts
if (navData?.autoNext && navData.next) {
      this.eventbus?.broadcast('request-video-url', [
        navData.next.videoUrlIndex,
      ]);
```

**Fix.** In `_handleVideoComplete`, replace the direct broadcast with `this._handleNavigateVideoUrl(navData.next.videoUrlIndex);`. That call highlights the menu item, broadcasts `request-video-url [nextIndex, 0]`, sets `activeNavigationPoint` and pushes history state. The existing spec at NavigationController.spec.ts:458 must change to expect `['request-video-url', [2, 0]]` instead of `[2]`. Also add assertions that `nav_2` has `current-menu-item` and `nav_1` does not, and that `controller.activeNavigationPoint === controller.navVidIdLookup[2]`.

Original suggestion: Call `this._handleNavigateVideoUrl(navData.next.videoUrlIndex)` so the highlight, active point and history update together.

**Verifier.** I traced the code and the main claim holds. In src/controllers/navigation-controller.ts:214-223, `_handleVideoComplete` broadcasts `request-video-url` with only `[navData.next.videoUrlIndex]`. It never calls `_highlightMenu`, never assigns `activeNavigationPoint` and never calls `_pushCurrentState`. The other two ways of changing items do update that state: - The menu click (`_menuMouseupHandler`) goes through `_handleNavigateVideoUrl`, which highlights, sets `activeNavigationPoint` and pushes history (lines 191-202). - RoutingController's popstate handler broadcasts `highlight-navigation` before `request-video-url` (routing-controller.ts:72-73).  I found nothing that resyncs the state after an autoNext. No listener anywhere in src reacts to `request-video-url` by broadcasting `highlight-navigation` or `navigate-to-video-url`. Nothing writes `activeNavigationPoint` except `_initHistory` and `_handleNavigateVideoUrl`. So after `video-complete [0]` with autoNext and next = item 1: - the `current-menu-item` class stays on `nav_0`; - `activeNavigationPoint` stays item 0; - `request-current-navigation` returns item 0; - no `push-history-state` is sent, so the URL hash keeps pointing at item 0.  The existing test ('should handle video-complete with autoNext enabled', NavigationController.spec.ts:446) only checks that `request-video-url` was broadcast with `[2]`. It does not check the highlight or the active point, so no test covers this.  Two things limit the impact: 1. Nothing in the engine broadcasts `video-complete` (no producer in src). No VideoPositionSource or other core code broadcasts `before-request-video-url` either. So the bug only shows when host code broadcasts the public, non-@private `video-complete` event. 2. The second half of the auditor's scenario overstates it. RoutingController only asks `request-current-navigation` from `_handleBeforeRequestVideoUrl`, and in the core code nothing ever triggers that. The real history effect is that no new history entry is pushed for item 1, so the hash stays at item 0 rather than being actively rewritten. RoutingController is also marked "work in progress, do not use".
