The stale highlight and stale `activeNavigationPoint` are real and follow deterministically once the event fires, so the verdict is confirmed at low severity.

**Repro test.** `given an attached NavigationController whose item at videoUrlIndex 1 has autoNext and next = item 2, when video-complete is broadcast with [1], then activeNavigationPoint is the item at videoUrlIndex 2 and only #nav_2 has the current-menu-item class`

### 46. [low] _buildNavigationData mutates shared json, breaking a second controller on it

[navigation-controller.ts:259](src/controllers/navigation-controller.ts#L259) · area: controllers · reported severity: low · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** Both NavigationController and RoutingController rewrite operationData.json in place: they delete nextId, add previous/next cycles, and replace root children ids with objects. When both controllers get the same json in one action's operation data, the second one resolves children by object key ('[object Object]') to undefined, and NavigationController then crashes rendering them. Children deeper than one level are never resolved from ids, so grandchildren are never rendered.

**Failure scenario.** In one action, add a RoutingController and then a NavigationController, with the json flowing through operation data. Routing turns root children into objects. Navigation maps them through navLookup[object], gets [undefined], and _addNavElement throws 'Cannot read properties of undefined (reading visible)' during attach. Separately, roots ['a'], a.children ['b'], b.children ['c']: 'c' stays a string and is never rendered.

**Evidence.**

```ts
data.roots.forEach((id: string) => {
      const nav = this.navLookup[id];
      if (nav.children) {
        nav.children = nav.children.map((childId: string) => {
          return this.navLookup[childId];
        });
      }
...
  private _addNavElement(parentElm: JQuery, data: any) {
    if (data.visible) {
```

**Fix.** In both controllers, build from deepCopy(operationData.json), or keep previous/next/children links in a separate Map, so the input is never changed. Resolve each children entry only when it is a string, and do it recursively for every node, not only the roots. Throw a named error for an unknown id, e.g. "NavigationController: unknown navigation id 'x' in children of 'a'". Move the shared logic into one helper, e.g. src/controllers/helper/build-navigation-data.ts.

Original suggestion: Build from a structural copy of json (or keep derived links in separate maps), resolve children recursively for every node, and validate unknown ids with a named error. Share one helper between both controllers instead of the duplicated _buildNavigationData.

**Verifier.** I traced the scenario through the real code and it holds. RoutingController.init (src/controllers/routing-controller.ts:24, _buildNavigationData at line 182) and NavigationController.init (navigation-controller.ts:35, _buildNavigationData at line 242) both change the json they are given in place. Each sets nav.previous and nav.next, deletes nav.nextId, and replaces the children of each root with nav objects.  The only guard is in Action.executeOperation, where copyConfiguredOperationData (src/action/action.ts) deep-copies an operation's configured data before merging it. That only protects json that is configured again on each operation. When json is set once (in the configuration, or by loadJson, which hands back its own copy) and then passed along the chain, both addControllerToElement calls in the action receive the same object. RoutingController runs first and turns a.children into [navObjB]. NavigationController then maps those through this.navLookup[obj], which looks up the key '[object Object]', so the children become [undefined]. In attach, _buildHtml calls _addNavElement(ul, undefined), and that throws on data.visible.  I confirmed this with a temporary vitest probe, since deleted: after both inits, NavigationController's navigation[0].children was [undefined], and attach() threw. There is a second effect. Because nextId has been deleted, the second controller sets nav.next = navigationData[index+1] and overwrites the next target that nextId chose.  The grandchildren claim also holds. Only root children are mapped from ids. _addNavElement recurses into a child's children, but those are still strings, so for a string 'c', data.visible is undefined and 'c' is never rendered.  Why the severity stays low: - RoutingController's JSDoc says "This is a work in progress, do not use this yet please". - The crash needs both controllers in one action with json shared through the chain. Giving json to each addControllerToElement avoids it, because each copy is deep-copied. - The one-level children limit looks like an old, undocumented limit of the data format, not a regression.  No existing test covers either controller receiving json that has already been processed.

**Repro test.** `given RoutingController initialised with a json whose root 'a' has children ['b'], when a NavigationController is initialised with the same json object and attached, then attach does not throw and renders an anchor for 'b'`

### 47. [low] Navigation menu items only respond to mouseup, not keyboard activation

[navigation-controller.ts:180](src/controllers/navigation-controller.ts#L180) · area: controllers · reported severity: low · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** Menu anchors get only a jQuery mouseup handler and href 'javascript:;'. Pressing Enter on a focused anchor fires click, not mouseup, so keyboard and assistive-technology users cannot navigate.

**Failure scenario.** Tab to a menu link and press Enter: the click event fires, no handler runs, and the navigation does not change.

**Evidence.**

```ts
private _addClickHandler(parentElm: JQuery, videoIndex: number) {
    parentElm.mouseup(this._menuMouseupHandler.bind(this, videoIndex));
  }
```

**Fix.** In `_addClickHandler`, replace `parentElm.mouseup(...)` with `parentElm.on('click', e => { e.preventDefault(); this._menuMouseupHandler(videoIndex); })` and rename the handler to `_menuActivateHandler`. Do not bind both mouseup and click: one mouse click fires both events, so the menu would broadcast `request-video-url` twice. Change the href to `#` (preventDefault stops the hash change), or render a `<button type="button">`. Update the comment in `detach()` that refers to mouseup handlers. Change the existing spec at NavigationController.spec.ts:382 to trigger `click`, and add a test that `mouseup` alone no longer navigates.

Original suggestion: Bind 'click' (with preventDefault) instead of mouseup, and drop the javascript: href in favour of href="#" or a <button>.

**Verifier.** I traced the code and the claim holds. In F:\projects\eligius\eligius\src\controllers\navigation-controller.ts, `_addNavElement` (lines 157-177) creates each menu item as `<a href="javascript:;" id="nav_N">`. Because the anchor has an href, it can receive focus with Tab. `_addClickHandler` (line 179-181) binds only `parentElm.mouseup(...)`, and no other code in the controller binds anything else. I found no click, keydown or keyup binding on the anchors or the `<ul>`, and no delegated handler on the container. When a focused anchor is activated with Enter, or through an accessibility API's default action (for example NVDA's activate, or a switch device), the browser sends a synthetic `click`, not `mouseup`. So `_menuMouseupHandler` never runs, `_handleNavigateVideoUrl` is never called, and neither `request-video-url` nor `push-history-state` is broadcast. The href is `javascript:;`, so the default click action does nothing either. Nothing guards against this, and nothing documents it as intended behaviour. The only test of activation, F:\projects\eligius\eligius\src\test\unit\controllers\NavigationController.spec.ts:382-390, triggers `mouseup` directly, so the keyboard path is untested. Mouse users and touch users are unaffected, since browsers fire compatibility mouseup events on a tap. The affected users are keyboard-only and assistive-technology users. NavigationController is an opt-in controller with no data loss and no engine-state corruption, so low severity is right.

**Repro test.** `given an attached NavigationController with navigation data, when a focused menu anchor is activated by keyboard (click event without mouseup), then request-video-url is broadcast exactly once with [videoUrlIndex, 0]`

### 48. [low] MutationObserverController.attach twice leaks the first observer

[mutation-observer-controller.ts:154](src/controllers/mutation-observer-controller.ts#L154) · area: controllers · reported severity: low · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** attach overwrites this.observer without disconnecting an existing one. The first observer keeps broadcasting 'dom-mutation' after detach, because detach only disconnects the latest one. The spec even notes that a guard was planned and never added.

**Failure scenario.** Call controller.attach(eventbus) twice, then controller.detach(eventbus), then change an attribute on the element. A 'dom-mutation' event is still broadcast by the orphaned first observer, for as long as the element lives.

**Evidence.**

```ts
this.observer = new MutationObserver(
      this._handleMutations.bind(this, eventbus, nativeElement)
    );
```

**Fix.** At the top of attach(), after the operationData and nativeElement guards, add `this.observer?.disconnect();` before creating the new observer. That way a re-attach (possibly with new init data) replaces the old observer instead of stacking a second one. Then change spec T027 so it attaches twice, detaches, mutates an attribute, flushes microtasks, and asserts that no 'dom-mutation' broadcast happened. Add a second check that, while still attached after the double attach, one mutation produces exactly one broadcast.

Original suggestion: At the start of attach, `this.observer?.disconnect()` (or return early when already attached). Turn the 'duplicate attach' spec into an assertion that only one observer fires.

**Verifier.** I traced the code and the leak is real. In src/controllers/mutation-observer-controller.ts, attach() (lines 139-160) only bails out when operationData or the native element is missing. It then always assigns `this.observer = new MutationObserver(...)` and calls observe(), without checking for or disconnecting an observer that already exists. detach() (lines 168-177) disconnects only the observer currently in `this.observer` and then sets it to null. After two attach calls, the first observer has no reference left on the controller. It stays registered on the element, and its bound callback still holds the eventbus, so every later mutation broadcasts 'dom-mutation'.  Nothing elsewhere prevents this: - addControllerToElement (src/operation/add-controller-to-element.ts) calls init and then attach on whatever controllerInstance it receives, with no check for an existing attachment. - attachControllerToElement only pushes the instance into the element's controller list, so duplicates are possible. - removeControllerFromElement removes and detaches only the first match by name.  The spec does not cover this. 'should handle duplicate attach calls safely' (mutation-observer-controller.spec.ts:339-355) only checks that both observers are non-null, and its comment says the guard was planned for 'Phase 6' and never added. No test checks behaviour after detach following a double attach.  Why the severity is low: in normal configurations each getControllerInstance call goes through 'request-instance' (engine-factory.ts:86), which creates a fresh instance, so one instance is attached once. A double attach needs direct API use, or an unusual but schema-valid chain: getControllerFromElement on an element that already has the controller, followed by addControllerToElement. That re-attaches the same instance and also pushes it into the element's controller list a second time.

**Repro test.** `given a MutationObserverController attached twice to the same element, when detach is called and an attribute on the element then changes, then no 'dom-mutation' event is broadcast`

### 49. [low] SubtitlesController.init throws an unnamed TypeError for empty or ragged subtitleData

[subtitles-controller.ts:113](src/controllers/subtitles-controller.ts#L113) · area: controllers · reported severity: low · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** createActionLookup indexes subtitleData[0] and subs.titles[i] without checks. An empty array, missing subtitleData, or one language with fewer titles than the first throws a raw TypeError that names neither the controller nor the bad entry. Line 144's optional chaining suggests a missing value was meant to be tolerated.

**Failure scenario.** subtitleData = [{languageCode:'en-US', titles:[t1,t2]}, {languageCode:'nl-NL', titles:[t1]}]. init throws 'Cannot read properties of undefined (reading text)' from inside addControllerToElement.

**Evidence.**

```ts
const subtitleData = operationData.subtitleData;
    const titles = subtitleData[0].titles;
...
        titleLanguageLookup[subs.languageCode] = subs.titles[i].text;
```

**Fix.** In createActionLookup (or at the start of init), treat missing or empty subtitleData as having no subtitles: set _titleRanges = [], subtitleDurations = [] and return {}. Validate before the loop. If any subtitleData[j].titles.length differs from subtitleData[0].titles.length, throw `SubtitlesController: subtitleData[${j}] (${languageCode}) has ${n} titles, expected ${m} (titles are matched by index across languages)`. Then drop the misleading `?.` on line 144, or make it consistent with the new empty-data handling.

Original suggestion: Validate in init: throw `SubtitlesController: subtitleData[<j>] (<code>) has <n> titles, expected <m>` and accept empty data as 'no subtitles'. Use `subs.titles[i]?.text ?? ''` if ragged data should be allowed.

**Verifier.** I traced the code and the failure holds. addControllerToElement (src/operation/add-controller-to-element.ts) calls controllerInstance.init(operationData) without checking anything first. SubtitlesController.init (src/controllers/subtitles-controller.ts:139-147) calls createActionLookup before its own line 144. createActionLookup reads subtitleData[0].titles at line 113, so empty or missing subtitleData throws "Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'titles')". That means the `?.` at line 144 never protects anything. Line 122 reads subs.titles[i].text for every language using the index of the first language's titles, so a language with fewer titles throws "reading 'text'". A language with more titles silently loses the extra ones.  I looked for a guard and found none: - ISubtitleCollection in src/types.ts:340 does not require the title arrays to have the same length. - subtitleData is typed only as array/object in the metadata tags, so the schema doesn't check it either. - No resolver or operation validates it. - SubtitlesController.spec.ts only uses well-formed createSubtitleData()/createHostileSubtitleData() and has no empty or ragged case.  One mitigation makes the summary slightly overstated. Since commit 68fe4110, withOperationContext in src/action/action.ts adds operationSystemName='addControllerToElement' and operationIndex to the error, and the engine `error` event reports them. So the operation is named, but the controller, the language and the bad entry are not. The error is still a raw TypeError. That goes against CLAUDE.md's rule that invalid configuration must fail fast with a message naming the JSON path.  The ragged case is clearly a configuration mistake: the arrays are matched by index across languages. Severity stays low. Nothing crashes beyond that action, and the engine reports it through the error path. The real cost is a confusing message and dead optional chaining.

**Repro test.** `given subtitleData where nl-NL has fewer titles than en-US, when SubtitlesController.init is called, then it throws an error naming SubtitlesController and subtitleData[1] (nl-NL) instead of a TypeError reading 'text'`

### 50. [low] createEngine that throws after registering leaves interceptor and listener on eventbus

[engine-factory.ts:184](src/engine-factory.ts#L184) · area: lens-lifecycle-memory · reported severity: low · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** The ActionRegistryEventbusListener and RequestVideoUriInterceptor are registered before resolver.process(), _createTimelineProviders() and _loadLocales(). Each of those can throw (unknown operation names and duplicate action names now fail fast, a provider type can lack a timeline, inline locales can be invalid). No destroy closure exists yet when that happens, so the registrations stay until factory.destroy().

**Failure scenario.** An editor previews a configuration with a typo in an operation name. createEngine throws 'unknown operation ...'. The user fixes the typo and createEngine runs again on the same factory. Every request-timeline-uri now broadcasts 'before-request-timeline-uri' twice (once per stacked interceptor), and the failed attempt's listener stays registered.

**Evidence.**

```ts
`addEngineRemover(this._eventbus.registerInterceptor('request-timeline-uri', new RequestVideoUriInterceptor(this._eventbus)));` followed by `const [actionLookup, resolvedConfiguration] = resolver.process(configuration, actionRegistryListener);` with no try/catch
```

**Fix.** Move resolver.process (and the _actionsLookup assignment) ahead of the interceptor registration. Keep the ActionRegistryEventbusListener construction before it, but register the listener on the eventbus only after process returns. The listener holds the event actions internally and receives nothing until it is registered, so a throw then leaves the eventbus untouched. Also wrap the rest of the method (timeline providers, engine construction, adapters) in try/catch: on failure, run engineRemovers and rethrow, so later failures such as _createTimelineProviders throwing do not leak either.

Original suggestion: Resolve the configuration and create the providers before registering on the eventbus, or wrap the body after the first addEngineRemover in try/catch and run engineRemovers before rethrowing.

**Verifier.** I traced the path through the real code and it holds. In src/engine-factory.ts, lines 177-189 of createEngine register two things on the shared this._eventbus before resolver.process is called at line 193: the ActionRegistryEventbusListener (only when eventActions is non-empty) and a RequestVideoUriInterceptor. Their removers go into the local engineRemovers array and into this._eventRemovers. Nothing wraps lines 191-247 in a try block. If process throws, engineRemovers stays local and is lost, and only factory.destroy() removes the registrations.  ConfigurationResolver.process (src/configuration/configuration-resolver.ts) throws in several places: - line 71: missing timelineProviderSettings - lines 204-212 (via resolveActions/resolveTimelines): unknown operation names - line 139: duplicate action names - lines 431/448: unknown names  In Eventbus.registerInterceptor (src/eventbus/eventbus.ts:166-177) the interceptors are pushed onto a list with no de-duplication. A failed call followed by a successful one therefore leaves two RequestVideoUriInterceptors, and 'before-request-timeline-uri' is broadcast twice per request.  There is a worse path. resolveEventActions (lines 155-171) calls actionRegistryListener.registerAction for every event action before createActionsLookup checks for duplicate names. A config where an event action's name matches a timeline action's name therefore leaves fully resolved event actions registered on a live listener. They run on later eventbus events even though createEngine threw.  I found no guard against this. The resolver is not run first, the eventbus does not de-duplicate interceptors, and no try/finally exists. The only spec that covers a throwing createEngine (engine-factory.spec.ts:178, the engine systemName import failure) throws at line 165, before any registration, so it does not exercise this path. No test checks eventbus state after a resolution failure.  The severity stays low. It needs an invalid configuration and then reuse of the same factory, for example an editor or live preview retrying after a typo. factory.destroy() still cleans everything up.

**Repro test.** `given a factory and a configuration whose eventAction name duplicates an action name, when createEngine throws, then broadcasting the event action's event does not run its operations and request-timeline-uri has no interceptor left registered`

### 51. [low] Factory _eventRemovers keeps every destroyed engine's remover closures

[engine-factory.ts:173](src/engine-factory.ts#L173) · area: lens-lifecycle-memory · reported severity: low · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** Per-engine removers are pushed into the factory-wide list as well, and nothing takes them out after the engine's destroy() has run them. The remover closures capture the ActionRegistryEventbusListener (and through it every event action with its resolved operations) and the interceptor. A reused factory therefore retains one listener graph per engine it has ever created.

**Failure scenario.** An SPA keeps one EngineFactory and creates and destroys an engine per route. After 50 navigations, factory._eventRemovers holds 100 closures retaining 50 ActionRegistryEventbusListener instances and all their event actions, even though each engine was destroyed correctly.

**Evidence.**

```ts
`const addEngineRemover = (remover: TEventbusRemover) => { engineRemovers.push(remover); this._eventRemovers.push(remover); };`  eventbus.ts registerEventlistener: `return () => { const index = this.eventListeners.indexOf(eventbusListener); ...`
```

**Fix.** In the destroy closure's finally block, after engineRemovers.forEach(remove => remove()), prune them from the factory list: `this._eventRemovers = this._eventRemovers.filter(r => !engineRemovers.includes(r)); engineRemovers.length = 0;`. Alternatively, make _eventRemovers a Set and delete each engine remover after calling it. Keep factory.destroy() calling the remaining ones.

Original suggestion: In the destroy closure, filter the engine's removers out of this._eventRemovers after calling them (or keep a Set and delete each one), so only live engines' removers stay referenced.

**Verifier.** I traced the claim through the code and it holds. In src/engine-factory.ts (lines 170-174), every per-engine remover is pushed to the local engineRemovers array and also to this._eventRemovers. Nothing removes entries from this._eventRemovers except factory.destroy() (lines 107-110), which runs them all and resets the array. The per-engine destroy closure (line 267) calls engineRemovers.forEach(remove => remove()) but does not prune this._eventRemovers. The remover closures from Eventbus.registerEventlistener (eventbus.ts 156-164) and registerInterceptor (166-179) capture the listener or interceptor object, and for the interceptor also the interceptors array. ActionRegistryEventbusListener keeps a _actionRegistry Map<string, IAction[]> (actionregistry-eventbus-listener.ts:9), filled by ConfigurationResolver with every resolved event action. RequestVideoUriInterceptor holds the eventbus.  So a reused factory keeps one interceptor and, for configurations with eventActions, one listener plus its event actions for every engine it has created. Running a remover a second time is harmless (indexOf returns -1), so the leak is memory only, with no behaviour change. The existing test 'given a reused factory, when an engine with event actions is destroyed, then its event actions and interceptor are removed' (engine-factory-lifecycle.spec.ts:369) only checks that they are gone from the eventbus. It does not check the factory's list, so no test covers this.  Severity stays low. The leak needs a long-lived factory that creates many engines. Each engine leaks memory in proportion to its configuration, not to DOM state, and factory.destroy() releases all of it.

**Repro test.** `given a reused EngineFactory and a configuration with eventActions, when an engine is created and its destroy() is awaited, then factory['_eventRemovers'] has the same length as before createEngine (only the 3 request handlers remain)`

### 52. [low] A second factory destroy() re-runs init action end operations

[eligius-engine.ts:713](src/eligius-engine.ts#L713) · area: lens-lifecycle-memory · reported severity: low · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** The factory closure guards only liveEngineCount with `destroyed`. It still calls engineInstance.destroy() every time. Init actions are EndableActions with no active guard, so their end operations run again. The adapters are already disconnected by then, so selectElement gets no root and the next operation throws.

**Failure scenario.** A React effect cleanup and an unmount handler both call the factory destroy(). An init action has endOperations [selectElement '.main', removeElement]. On the second call request('request-engine-root') returns undefined, removeElement throws 'Cannot read properties of undefined', and the second destroy() rejects. Any broadcastEvent end operation fires twice.

**Evidence.**

```ts
engine-factory.ts: `await engineInstance.destroy(); } finally { ... if (!destroyed) {`  eligius-engine.ts destroy: `await this._endActionsSettled([...(this.configuration.initActions ?? [])].reverse(), errors);`  endable-action.ts: `end(initOperationData?) { if (!this.endOperations.length) {... return this._runOperations(this.endOperations, ...)`
```

**Fix.** In `_initializeTimelineProvider`, record each init action in `this._startedInitActions` only after its start() resolves. One option is to start them in a loop that pushes after each await instead of calling `_executeActions` on the whole list. In destroy(), end only `[...this._startedInitActions].reverse()` and then clear the list. Add `private _destroyed = false`, set it at the top of destroy(), and return early when it is already set, so the end operations and teardown run once. Optionally, the factory closure can memoize its destroy promise so a second call returns the first call's promise instead of running `engineInstance.destroy()` and the adapter disconnects again.

Original suggestion: Make the factory closure return the first destroy promise on later calls (`destroyPromise ??= (async () => {...})()`), and/or give EligiusEngine.destroy() a _destroyed guard that turns repeat calls into no-ops.

**Verifier.** I traced the code and found no guard.  1. EligiusEngine.destroy() (src/eligius-engine.ts:694-773) calls `_endActionsSettled([...initActions].reverse(), errors)` with no check that the actions started. The only place init actions start is `_initializeTimelineProvider()` (line 585: `_executeActions(initActions, 'start')`). That call is skipped when `localesReady` rejects, when there are no timelines, when no provider is found, or when `_activateProvider` throws. If one init action fails partway through `_executeActions`, the later init actions also never start. 2. EndableAction.end() (src/action/endable-action.ts) does not track whether the action started. Its only check is `endOperations.length`, so it always runs the end operations. 3. Nothing makes destroy() idempotent. There is no `_destroyed` flag. The factory closure (src/engine-factory.ts:254-277) uses its `destroyed` flag only for `liveEngineCount`/`clearGlobals`. It calls `engineInstance.destroy()` on every call and disconnects the adapters in `finally` each time.  Consequences: - **Failed init.** The usual end operations are mostly tolerant. `selectElement` only warns on an empty match, and `removeControllerFromElement` warns when the controller is missing. So a first destroy after a failed init usually just logs spurious warnings and runs end operations whose start never happened. It rejects only when an end operation depends on state the start created, such as an endAction target or data set by a start operation. - **Second destroy (concrete failure).** The first call removes the engine's eventbus listeners, and the factory disconnects EngineEventbusAdapter, which serves `request-engine-root` via `_registerRequest` in engine-eventbus-adapter.ts:168. On the second call, `selectElement` gets `undefined` from `eventbus.request('request-engine-root')` and leaves `selectedElement` unset. `removeControllerFromElement` then calls `getElementControllers(undefined)`, which evaluates `undefined.data(...)` and throws a TypeError, so the second destroy() rejects. The factory's own `destroyed` flag shows that repeat calls are meant to be tolerated.  Tests: eligius-engine.spec.ts only checks that init actions end in reverse order after a successful init (line 763) and that end operations that throw are collected (line 1994). No test covers destroy after a failed init or destroying twice.  Severity stays low. Teardown is noisy or rejects, but playback and engine state are unaffected, and both triggers are edge paths: a failed init followed by destroy, or a double destroy.

**Repro test.** `given an engine whose init() rejected because localesReady rejected, when destroy() is awaited, then no init action end operation is called; and given a factory-created engine with an init action ending in selectElement + removeControllerFromElement that was destroyed once, when the returned destroy() is called a second time, then it resolves without running the end operations again`

### 53. [low] Factory engines lose end-operation error details unless engine-error is heard

[endable-action.ts:23](src/action/endable-action.ts#L23) · area: engine-core · reported severity: low · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** The engine logs an action error only when it has no `error` subscribers. EngineEventbusAdapter always subscribes, so for factory-created engines the error goes only to the 'engine-error' broadcast, and nothing in the library listens to that. EndableAction.end logs just the action name, not the error. An end-operation failure therefore leaves only "Error in action end 'X'" in the console. Start failures are logged twice instead: Action.start logs e, and the engine logs again for direct engines.

**Failure scenario.** In an EngineFactory engine, a timeline action's end operation throws TypeError('x is undefined'). The console shows only "Error in action end 'FadeOut'", with no message, failing operation or stack, because the EngineActionError went to an eventbus event with no listeners.

**Evidence.**

```ts
return this._runOperations(this.endOperations, initOperationData).catch(
      e => {
        console.error(`Error in action end '${this.name}'`);
        throw e;
      }
    );
...
    if (this._errorSubscriptions.size > 0) {
      this._emitter.emit('error', error);
    } else {
      console.error(error.message);
    }
```

**Fix.** Do not count the adapter as a consumer. Add an internal way for EngineEventbusAdapter to forward errors that does not add to _errorSubscriptions, for example an `{internal: true}` subscription flag or a separate `onErrorForward` hook. Alternatively, have the adapter check whether the eventbus has any 'engine-error' handler or listener and call console.error(error.message, error.cause) when it has none. Separately, make EndableAction.end log `e` the way Action.start does, or drop the console logging from both. If you drop it from both, the engine fallback must log error.cause as well as error.message so the stack is kept.

Original suggestion: Remove console logging from Action/EndableAction, since callers now get annotated errors. Have EngineEventbusAdapter (or the engine) log the EngineActionError when the eventbus has no 'engine-error' listener, or do not count the adapter's subscription in `_errorSubscriptions`.

**Verifier.** I traced the claim through the code and it holds. 1) EngineFactory.createEngine (src/engine-factory.ts:229-245) always creates and connects an EngineEventbusAdapter. Its connect() calls engine.on('error', ...) (src/adapters/engine-eventbus-adapter.ts:97-99). That on() adds an entry to _errorSubscriptions (src/eligius-engine.ts:340-345). 2) Timeline action ends go through _runActionMethod(action, 'end') (eligius-engine.ts:987, 1148, 1168) and from there to _reportActionError (1009-1021). Because _errorSubscriptions.size > 0 for every factory engine, that method takes the emit branch and never reaches console.error(error.message). 3) The adapter broadcasts 'engine-error'. Eventbus.broadcast leads to _callHandlers (eventbus.ts:234-241), which does nothing when no handlers are registered. It does not log or call a fallback. Nothing in src/ outside tests listens for 'engine-error'. 4) EndableAction.end (src/action/endable-action.ts:21-26) logs only "Error in action end '<name>'" and not the error itself. Action.start (action.ts:33-36) also logs `e`, so start failures stay visible and end failures do not. I found no guard that rescues this. The `on()` JSDoc says errors are logged only "while it has no subscribers", and that is true at the engine level. But CHANGELOG.md line 15 says "Without listeners the message is logged", and for factory engines with no eventbus listener that is false. The integration test at src/test/integration/engine-factory-lifecycle.spec.ts:440 covers only a start failure with an explicit engine.on('error') subscriber. It also mocks console.error, so the lost message is never checked. No test covers an end failure in a factory engine with no listener. Severity stays low. No behaviour or state is wrong; the only effect is a less useful log. A host that listens to 'engine-error' or engine 'error' gets the full EngineActionError.

**Repro test.** `given an EngineFactory engine with no engine-error listener and a timeline action whose end operation throws, when the clock passes the action end, then console.error is called with a message containing "Error in action end 'Failing'" and the failing operation name and cause message`

### 54. [low] getFormData merges form fields with inherited Object.prototype names

[get-form-data.ts:57](src/operation/get-form-data.ts#L57) · area: lens-security-robustness · reported severity: low · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** `formData` is a plain `{}`, so `formData[field.name] !== undefined` is true for inherited members such as `constructor`, `toString` and `__proto__`. These fields are turned into arrays that contain the inherited function, or written to the prototype.

**Failure scenario.** <form><input name="constructor" value="x"></form> gives formData.constructor === [Object, 'x'] instead of 'x' (probe-confirmed; likewise toString -> [fn, 'y']). A field named __proto__ sets formData's prototype and its value is lost.

**Evidence.**

```ts
const formData: Record<string, any> = {};
  for (const field of formArray) {
    if (formData[field.name] !== undefined) {
      if (!Array.isArray(formData[field.name])) {
        formData[field.name] = [formData[field.name]];
      }
      formData[field.name].push(field.value);
```

**Fix.** Build formData with `Object.create(null)` (or a Map converted with Object.fromEntries), and replace the `!== undefined` check with `Object.hasOwn(formData, field.name)`. With a null-prototype object, `__proto__` becomes an ordinary own key. If downstream code expects a normal prototype, keep `{}`, use Object.hasOwn, and set the value with `Object.defineProperty(formData, field.name, {value, enumerable: true, writable: true, configurable: true})` so the `__proto__` setter is never invoked. Apply the same change to validateForm's `validationErrors[name]`.

Original suggestion: Use `Object.hasOwn(formData, field.name)` for the duplicate check and create formData with `Object.create(null)`, or skip forbidden names. The same pattern applies to validateForm's `validationErrors[name]`.

**Verifier.** I traced the code in src/operation/get-form-data.ts (lines 52-66) and found no guard. `formData` is a plain `{}`, and the duplicate check `formData[field.name] !== undefined` reads inherited members. Nothing upstream filters field names: jQuery's serializeArray only skips fields that have no name, are disabled, or have an unsubmittable type. The only caller is the operation registry in src/operation/index.ts. The spec at src/test/unit/operation/get-form-data.spec.ts has no case for constructor, toString, __proto__ or hasOwn.  I ran the same loop in Node and got the reported results: - `constructor` gives `[Function Object, 'x']`. - `toString` gives `[Function toString, 'y']`. - `__proto__` takes the "already defined" branch and assigns `[Object.prototype]` through the `__proto__` setter. formData's prototype becomes an array, the value is pushed onto that array, and formData ends up with no own keys (JSON shows `{}`), so the value is lost.  The global Object.prototype is not polluted. The probe showed `({}).z` is undefined, so the impact is limited to this result object. The trigger is a page author's form field name, and only unusual names like these cause it. Real impact: wrong or missing formData values for fields named after Object.prototype members. Severity low is correct.

**Repro test.** `given a form with an input named "constructor" valued "x" and an input named "__proto__" valued "z", when getFormData runs, then formData.constructor equals "x" and Object.hasOwn(formData, "__proto__") is true with value "z"`

### 55. [low] Eventbus.off(name, handler) cannot remove a handler registered with once()

[eventbus.ts:109](src/eventbus/eventbus.ts#L109) · area: eventbus-locale-util · reported severity: low · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** once() stores a private wrapper, so off() with the original handler finds nothing and silently does nothing. The handler still fires on the next broadcast. TypedEventEmitter.once (src/util/typed-event-emitter.ts:106) behaves the same way. off() is a public IEventbus method, and nothing in its documentation says it ignores once() registrations.

**Failure scenario.** A controller calls eventbus.once('timeline-complete', this.onDone) in attach and eventbus.off('timeline-complete', this.onDone) in detach. After detach and before the timeline completes, onDone still runs on a detached controller.

**Evidence.**

```ts
const eventHandlerDecorator = (...args: any[]) => { ... };
    this.on(eventName, eventHandlerDecorator, eventTopic);
...
  off(...) {
    const handlers = this._getEventHandlers(eventName, eventTopic);
    ...
      const idx = handlers.indexOf(eventHandler);
```

**Fix.** In Eventbus.once, attach the original handler to the wrapper: `(eventHandlerDecorator as any).original = eventHandler`. In Eventbus.off, find the index with `handlers.findIndex(h => h === eventHandler || (h as any).original === eventHandler)`. Match an exact registration first so that off(wrapper) still behaves as it does today. Make the same change in TypedEventEmitter.once and TypedEventEmitter.off. When off() matches through `.original`, the wrapper's `called` flag does not need to change: once it is spliced out of the array, a later broadcast cannot reach it. A broadcast whose snapshot was already taken can still call it, which is the same as off() on a regular handler mid-dispatch today. Add a test to src/test/unit/eventbus/eventbus.spec.ts and one to src/test/unit/util/typed-event-emitter.spec.ts. Optionally document in IEventbus.off that it also removes handlers registered with once().

Original suggestion: Keep the original handler on the wrapper (for example eventHandlerDecorator.original = eventHandler). In off(), match handlers[i] === eventHandler || handlers[i].original === eventHandler. Make the same change in TypedEventEmitter.once/off. Add a test for once() followed by off() with the original handler.

**Verifier.** The code does what the report says. Eventbus.once (src/eventbus/eventbus.ts:109-132) registers a closure, `eventHandlerDecorator`, through on(). Eventbus.off (134-146) removes by `handlers.indexOf(eventHandler)`. Called with the original handler, indexOf returns -1, so off() silently does nothing and the wrapper fires on the next broadcast. TypedEventEmitter.once (src/util/typed-event-emitter.ts:106-113) has the same problem: it registers `onceHandler`, and off() (120-130) also matches with indexOf. Neither class marks the wrapper with the original handler, so off(original) cannot find it.  Reasons to refute, and why they fail: - The intended removal path is the remover that once() returns. That path works and is tested: eventbus.spec.ts:44 and typed-event-emitter.spec.ts:106. But off() is a public method on IEventbus and on ITypedEventEmitter. TypedEventEmitter documents it as "Event handler to remove", and neither interface says once() registrations are excluded. The common emitters (Node EventEmitter, jQuery .one/.off, DOM removeEventListener with {once:true}) all let you remove a once handler by passing the original function, so callers will expect it. - No test covers once() followed by off(original), in either eventbus.spec.ts or typed-event-emitter.spec.ts.  What limits severity: no production code in src/ calls `.once(` at all; every match is in src/test. Controllers use BaseController.addListener, which goes through on() and its remover. So no valid JSON configuration can trigger this, and the controller scenario in the report is hypothetical. It only affects consumers who call the exported Eventbus or the engine emitter directly and pair once() with off(). The impact is a stale callback firing once more, not state corruption. Low is the right severity.

**Repro test.** `given a handler registered with once(), when off() is called with that same handler and the event is then broadcast, then the handler is not called`

### 56. [low] Interceptors and listeners iterate live arrays; self-removal skips the next

[eventbus.ts:247](src/eventbus/eventbus.ts#L247) · area: eventbus-locale-util · reported severity: low · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** The fix round made handler dispatch iterate a snapshot, but interceptors and eventbus listeners are still iterated over the live arrays, and their removers splice those same arrays. An interceptor or listener that removes itself (or an earlier one) during a broadcast makes the for-of skip the entry that moves into the freed slot.

**Failure scenario.** Two interceptors are registered for 'request-timeline-uri': a one-shot interceptor A whose intercept() calls its own remover, and RequestVideoUriInterceptor B. On the first broadcast A splices index 0, B moves to index 0, the iterator advances to index 1 and B never runs. 'before-request-timeline-uri' is not broadcast for that request. A listener that unregisters itself in handleEvent has the same effect: the next listener, for example the engine's ActionRegistryEventbusListener, does not see the event.

**Evidence.**

```ts
for (const interceptor of interceptors) {
        try {
          args = interceptor.intercept(args);
...
      for (const listener of this.eventListeners) {
...
    return () => {
      const index = interceptors.indexOf(interceptor);
      if (index > -1) {
        interceptors.splice(index, 1);
```

**Fix.** In `_callHandlers`, iterate snapshots, as the handler loop already does: `for (const interceptor of interceptors.slice())` and `for (const listener of this.eventListeners.slice())`. You can take the handler snapshot at the same point for consistency. Add two tests next to the existing handler self-removal tests in src/test/unit/eventbus/eventbus.spec.ts: one with an interceptor that removes itself during dispatch, and one with a listener that does the same. A follow-up broadcast should then not call the removed one again.

Original suggestion: Iterate interceptors.slice() and this.eventListeners.slice(), as is already done for handlers. Add tests for an interceptor and for a listener that each remove themselves during dispatch.

**Verifier.** I traced the report through the real code in F:\projects\eligius\eligius\src\eventbus\eventbus.ts and it holds. The interceptor loop (line 247) iterates the live array returned by `_getEventInterceptors`, and the listener loop (line 255) iterates the live `this.eventListeners`. Each remover splices that same array: the `registerInterceptor` remover at lines 173-178 and the `registerEventlistener` remover at lines 158-163. A for-of over an array is index-based, so splicing index i during the visit to index i shifts the next entry into slot i and the loop then reads i+1, skipping it. Handlers alone are protected, by the snapshot at line 271. That fix and its tests (eventbus.spec.ts around lines 239 and 252) cover handlers only.  I found no guard elsewhere: - `clear()` replaces the arrays instead of splicing them, so it is unaffected. - No test covers an interceptor or listener that removes itself or an earlier entry during dispatch. The existing tests only remove them outside a broadcast (spec lines 98-118 and 202-220).  Why the severity stays low: - **No configuration trigger.** No operation, controller, adapter or configuration path can call `registerInterceptor` or `registerEventlistener`, or their removers. - **Internal registrants never remove themselves.** The only internal callers are in engine-factory.ts (lines 176-189): `ActionRegistryEventbusListener` and `RequestVideoUriInterceptor`. Neither removes itself or anything else during `handleEvent` or `intercept`. - **Only custom code can hit it.** Both methods are on the public `IEventbus` exported from index.ts, so the bug needs consumer code that registers a self-removing (one-shot) interceptor or listener on the shared eventbus. If it does, the next entry is skipped for that broadcast, for example `RequestVideoUriInterceptor` or the engine's `ActionRegistryEventbusListener`. - **Self-correcting.** The skip lasts only that one broadcast, because the array is consistent again afterwards.  This is a real but narrow public-API inconsistency.

**Repro test.** `given an interceptor that removes itself during dispatch and a second interceptor registered after it, when the event is broadcast, then the second interceptor is still called (and likewise: given a listener that unregisters itself in handleEvent and a second listener, when the event is broadcast, then the second listener's handleEvent is still called)`

### 57. [low] LocaleManager 'change' emit is not error-isolated and visits handlers added mid-emit

[locale-manager.ts:315](src/locale/locale-manager.ts#L315) · area: eventbus-locale-util · reported severity: low · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** _emit calls each change handler directly over a live Set. A throwing handler stops every later handler and throws out of setLocale after _locale and rosetta's locale have already changed. A handler added during the emit is also called for the same change, because Set iteration visits new entries. The codebase's own TypedEventEmitter already isolates errors and iterates a copy.

**Failure scenario.** localeManager.on('change', () => { throw new Error('x'); }) is registered before another change subscriber. setLocale('nl-NL') updates the locale, then throws to the caller. The second subscriber never learns the locale changed, so its UI stays in the old language while t() already returns Dutch.

**Evidence.**

```ts
const handlers = this._listeners.get(event);
    if (handlers) {
      for (const handler of handlers) {
        (handler as (...args: LocaleEvents[K]) => void)(...args);
      }
    }
```

**Fix.** In `_emit`, iterate over a snapshot (`[...handlers]`) and wrap each call in try/catch, logging with console.error and naming the event, the same way TypedEventEmitter does. The other option is to back LocaleManager's events with `TypedEventEmitter<LocaleEvents>` and keep the unsubscribe-function API of `on()`. Add two specs: one where a throwing change handler is registered before a second handler, and one where a handler is added from inside a change handler.

Original suggestion: Back LocaleManager's events with TypedEventEmitter<LocaleEvents>, which iterates a copy and catches and logs per handler, or copy the Set before iterating and wrap each call in try/catch with console.error. Add tests for a throwing change handler and for a handler added during emit.

**Verifier.** The quoted code matches src/locale/locale-manager.ts lines 309-319. `_emit` loops directly over the live `Set` from `_listeners`, with no copy and no try/catch. `setLocale` (lines 236-260) assigns `this._locale` and calls `this._rosetta.locale(locale)` before it calls `_emit('change', ...)`. So if a change handler throws, every later handler is skipped and the exception propagates out of `setLocale` after the state has already changed. The debug log is skipped too. The mid-emit part is also true: a JS `Set` iterator visits entries added during iteration, so a handler registered from inside a change handler runs for the same change. Nothing guards against either case. `TypedEventEmitter` (src/util/typed-event-emitter.ts:150-155) copies its handlers and catches per handler, so this emitter is inconsistent with the codebase's own pattern.  I found two reasons it is limited, but neither removes it. (1) The only internal subscriber is `LocaleEventbusAdapter`. Its handler sets the root `lang` attribute and calls `eventbus.broadcast`, and `Eventbus` catches listener errors (eventbus.ts:246-281). So no configuration can produce a throwing change handler, and eventbus `language-change` listeners are already isolated. (2) The failure needs consumer code that subscribes with `localeManager.on('change', ...)` directly. `LocaleManager` and `ILocaleManager.on` are public (exported from src/locale/index.ts and returned by `createEngine`), so a consumer can do this. The locale-manager spec has no test for a throwing change handler or for a handler added during emit; the only throw test covers a translation function in `t()`. Severity stays low: it needs a misbehaving consumer handler and cannot be triggered from a configuration.

**Repro test.** `given a 'change' handler that throws registered before a second 'change' handler, when setLocale('nl-NL') is called, then setLocale does not throw and the second handler is called with ('nl-NL', 'en-US')`

### 58. [low] LocaleLoader accepts non-object JSON and fails with an error that does not name the file

[locale-loader.ts:153](src/locale/locale-loader.ts#L153) · area: eventbus-locale-util · reported severity: low · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** response.json() is cast to TLocaleData without checking its shape. A file containing null crashes inside _resolveNestedRefs with a TypeError that does not name the file. A string or array is turned into index-keyed translations without any error.

**Failure scenario.** A misconfigured CDN serves en-US.json as `null`. Object.keys(null) throws 'Cannot convert undefined or null to object'. EngineFactory wraps it, but the message never says the file is not an object. With a JSON string body '"oops"', load() resolves with data {0:'o',1:'o',...}, loadLocale accepts it, and every label for that locale is ''.

**Evidence.**

```ts
const data = await response.json();
      return data as TLocaleData;
...
    const data = deepCopy(await this._fetchWithCache(url));
    await this._resolveNestedRefs(data, url, visitedPaths);
```

**Fix.** In `_doFetch`, after `await response.json()`, add `if (data === null || typeof data !== 'object' || Array.isArray(data)) throw new Error(`[LocaleLoader] Locale file '${url}' must contain a JSON object, got ${data === null ? 'null' : Array.isArray(data) ? 'array' : typeof data}`);`. Because the message contains `[LocaleLoader]`, the existing catch rethrows it unchanged. Since the check sits in `_doFetch`, it also covers nested `$ref` files, and the error names the nested URL rather than the root one. Add tests for `null`, array, string and number payloads, at root level and as a nested `$ref`.

Original suggestion: In _doFetch, after parsing, throw `[LocaleLoader] Locale file '${url}' must contain a JSON object` unless the value is a non-null, non-array object. Add tests for null, array and string payloads.

**Verifier.** I traced both payloads through the code and ran a throwaway probe test, now deleted. Nothing on the path validates the shape of the parsed JSON.  **`null` body.** - `_doFetch` (locale-loader.ts:153-154) returns `null` cast to `TLocaleData`. - `deepCopy(null)` returns `null`. ts-is-present's `isDefined` only rejects `undefined`, and `copyValue` passes primitives through unchanged. - `_resolveNestedRefs(null, ...)` then calls `Object.keys(null)` at line 197. That throws a bare TypeError: "Cannot convert undefined or null to object". - This happens in `_loadWithVisited`, outside `_doFetch`'s try/catch, so the error never gets the `[LocaleLoader] ... '<url>'` prefix. The probe confirmed the loader's message is exactly the raw TypeError.  One correction to the report: `EngineFactory._loadLocales` (engine-factory.ts:315-324) does wrap it as `[EngineFactory] Failed to load locale 'en-US' from '<root $ref>': Cannot convert...`. So at engine level the top-level file is named. The message still never says the payload is not an object. If the `null` is in a nested `$ref` file, the error names only the root ref, not the file that is actually broken.  **String body (`"oops"`).** - `deepCopy` returns `'oops'`. - `Object.keys('oops')` gives `['0','1','2','3']`. None of the characters is a reference, so `load()` resolves with `{locale:'en-US', data:'oops'}`. This also corrects the report: `load()` returns the raw string, not an index-keyed object. - `LocaleManager.loadLocale` → `isLocaleReference('oops')` is false. Then `mergeLocaleData(undefined, 'oops')` spreads the characters into `{0:'o',1:'o',2:'p',3:'s'}`, stores that in rosetta and marks the locale as available. - The probe confirmed `lm.t('greeting')` returns `""`. The failure is silent.  **What I checked for a guard.** There is no shape check in `_doFetch`, `deepCopy`, `_resolveNestedRefs`, `isLocaleReference`, `mergeLocaleData` or `EngineFactory._loadLocales`. locale-loader.spec.ts only tests object payloads, a 404, circular refs and diamond refs. It has no tests for `null`, array or primitive bodies.  **Severity.** The bug is real but stays low. It needs a misconfigured or broken server rather than a bad Eligius configuration. The `null` case already fails fast, with the top-level ref named by EngineFactory. Only the primitive and array cases silently produce empty labels.

**Repro test.** `given a locale file whose JSON body is the string "oops", when LocaleLoader.load() is called, then it rejects with an error naming the file and saying it must contain a JSON object`

### 59. [low] Starts from a later update can run before earlier starts that are waiting on slow ends

[eligius-engine.ts:1142](src/eligius-engine.ts#L1142) · area: lens-performance · reported severity: low · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** Ordering is only enforced within one update: an update's starts wait for that update's ends. An update with no ends runs its starts immediately, even when starts from an earlier update are still pending behind a slow end. Actions can therefore start out of timeline order.

**Failure scenario.** tickInterval 100. A=[2,5) has an end op animate 400ms (fade-out). B=[5,8) creates #panel with createElement. C=[5.1,8) runs selectElement '#panel' then addClass 'x'. Tick 5.0: B waits for A's end. Tick 5.1: no ends, so C starts at once. selectElement warns that '#panel' was not found and addClass works on an empty set. 400 ms later B creates #panel without the class.

**Evidence.**

```ts
if (!ends.length) {
      this._runPendingStarts(starts);
      return;
    }

    const endsSettled = ends.map(action =>
      this._runActionMethod(action, 'end')
    );
    if (starts.length) {
      Promise.all(endsSettled).then(() => this._runPendingStarts(starts));
    }
```

**Fix.** Keep a start gate on the engine, as proposed, but keep the common no-wait path synchronous. Existing tests rely on starts firing synchronously within `source.report()` when nothing is waiting.  1. Add the fields `_startGate: Promise<void> = Promise.resolve()` and `_waitingStartBatches = 0`. 2. Run synchronously when nothing waits. In `_dispatchActionsBetween`, if `ends` is empty and `_waitingStartBatches === 0`, call `_runPendingStarts(starts)` directly, as today. 3. Otherwise, chain the batch onto the gate:    ```ts    const gen = this._gateGeneration;    this._waitingStartBatches++;    this._startGate = Promise.all([this._startGate, ...endsSettled]).then(() => {      if (gen !== this._gateGeneration) return;      this._waitingStartBatches--;      this._runPendingStarts(starts);    });    ```    Do this only when `starts.length` is non-zero; ends that have no starts don't need to block later updates. 4. Reset the gate everywhere `_pendingStarts.clear()` is called (seek, stop, switch, timeline complete/cleanup, destroy). Increment `_gateGeneration`, set `_waitingStartBatches = 0` and set `_startGate = Promise.resolve()`. Batches that are already in flight then become no-ops, which `_runPendingStarts`' identity check already ensures for each individual start.

Original suggestion: Keep a single start-gate promise on the engine. Each update's start batch waits for the previous gate and for its own ends, then becomes the new gate. Reset the gate wherever _pendingStarts is cleared (seek, stop, switch, cleanup, destroy).

**Verifier.** I traced the scenario through src/eligius-engine.ts (`_onTimeHandler`, then `_dispatchActionsBetween` at lines 1086-1153, then `_runPendingStarts` at 1159) and it holds. I found no guard that prevents it.  - **Report 3:** A starts. - **Report 5:** the forward loop over (3, 5] pushes A's end event into `ends`, because A is active. It calls `addStart(B)`, which puts B in `_pendingStarts`. Since `ends` is non-empty, B's start is deferred behind `Promise.all(endsSettled)`. A's end runs `animate`, which resolves only in jQuery's complete callback (src/operation/animate.ts), so the defer lasts about 400 ms. - **Report 5.1:** the loop over (5, 5.1] contains only C's start event. `ends` is empty, so `_runPendingStarts([C])` runs synchronously and C starts at once. - **Cancel checks:** `_runPendingStarts` only checks whether each start in its own batch was cancelled. It never checks whether `_pendingStarts` still holds starts from an earlier update. - **Result:** C runs `selectElement('#panel')` before B has run `createElement`. select-element.ts line 11 warns "selector '#panel' wasn't found!", and `addClass` works on an empty set. When A's end settles, B creates #panel without the class.  Why it is not refuted: - **Documentation:** the doc comment on `_dispatchActionsBetween` and the `_pendingStarts` field comment only promise "within one update all ends run before any start". That limits the guarantee but does not state that later starts may overtake earlier ones on purpose. - **Existing tests:** the related tests in src/test/unit/eligius-engine.spec.ts are 'given a start waiting for a slow end, when stop() is called...' (line 2406) and '...when a later update passes its end...' (line 2425). Both cover a single action's pending start. Neither covers a different action's start in a later update with no ends, so no test locks in either behaviour. - **Partly a regression:** the old engine ran ends and starts concurrently, so B would have started at 5.0, before C.  Mitigations: - Starts were never awaited against each other even within one batch. Only the order in which they are fired is affected. - Triggering it needs a slow (animated) end op, two starts in consecutive ticks less than the end's duration apart, and the later start depending on the earlier one. - The failure is a console warning plus a missing class, not a crash or corrupted engine state.  Low severity is right.

**Repro test.** `given A=[2,5] with a slow end, B=[5,8] and C=[5.1,8], when positions 3, 5 and 5.1 are reported before A's end settles, then B-start is logged before C-start`

### 60. [low] HTTP ops send duplicate Content-Type when caller uses a different header case

[http-post.ts:65](src/operation/http-post.ts#L65) · area: operations-b · reported severity: low · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** httpPost and httpPut spread caller headers over a hard-coded 'Content-Type' key. Header names are case-insensitive, but object keys are not. A caller's 'content-type' becomes a second entry, and Headers appends both values. The body is also always JSON.stringify'd, so a caller who overrides the content type still sends JSON (a string body becomes a quoted string).

**Failure scenario.** httpPost {url, body:'hello', headers:{'content-type':'text/plain'}} sends 'Content-Type: application/json, text/plain' (I confirmed the Headers merge) with body '"hello"'. Strict servers reject or misparse the request.

**Evidence.**

```ts
http-post.ts:63-69: `headers: { 'Content-Type': 'application/json', ...headers, }, body: JSON.stringify(body),` (http-put.ts:63-69 is identical)
```

**Fix.** Move request construction into a shared helper, e.g. src/operation/helper/build-json-request.ts. It should do `const h = new Headers(headers); if (!h.has('content-type')) h.set('content-type', 'application/json');`, then pass `body: typeof body === 'string' && !/json/i.test(h.get('content-type') ?? '') ? body : JSON.stringify(body)`. Use it from both httpPost and httpPut. Add specs for http-post and http-put that check the fetch call's headers when the caller uses a lowercase key.

Original suggestion: Build a Headers object from the caller headers and set 'Content-Type: application/json' only when !headers.has('content-type'). Only JSON.stringify the body when the effective content type is JSON (or the body is not a string). Share this logic between httpPost and httpPut.

**Verifier.** I traced the code and could not refute the report. src/operation/http-post.ts:63-70 and src/operation/http-put.ts:63-70 pass a plain object literal, `{'Content-Type': 'application/json', ...headers}`, to fetch, and the body is always `JSON.stringify(body)`. Nothing upstream normalises the case of header keys. Both data interfaces type headers as `Record<string, string>` with no guard, and removeProperties only runs after the request. When fetch builds a Headers object from a record, it appends each entry in turn, so a key whose case differs is merged rather than replaced. I checked this in Node, which uses undici and follows the Fetch spec: - `new Headers({'Content-Type':'application/json','content-type':'text/plain'}).get('content-type')` gives `application/json, text/plain`. - A Request built with the operation's exact shape and a lowercase `'content-type': 'application/json'` sends `application/json, application/json`, with body `"hello"` for a string body.  You can hit this from a valid configuration just by writing the header in lowercase, which is common. Even when the caller asks for the same media type, the request carries a duplicated value. Strict parsers reject a duplicated value like this, and body-parser/type-is then skip the body. The only mitigation is that the TypeDoc examples use the exact 'Content-Type' casing, and that path works. No test covers this: the 'content-type' entries in http-post.spec.ts and http-put.spec.ts are on mocked response headers, and no test checks the request headers when the caller supplies a different case. The JSON.stringify half is arguably intended, since the op is documented as a JSON client. Still, a caller who overrides the content type to text/plain also gets a quoted string as the body. Severity stays low: the fault only shows with particular servers, and the workaround (use the exact casing) is trivial.

**Repro test.** `given headers {'content-type': 'application/json'} in lowercase, when httpPost is called, then the request sent to fetch has a single Content-Type value 'application/json' (currently 'application/json, application/json')`

### 61. [low] replaceString replaces only the first match, despite being documented as replaceText alias

[replace-string.ts:53](src/operation/replace-string.ts#L53) · area: operations-b · reported severity: low · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** replaceString uses String.replace with a string pattern, which replaces only the first occurrence and expands $&, $1 and similar in the replacement. replaceText splits and joins, so it replaces every occurrence and inserts the replacement literally. The doc calls replaceString an 'alias for replaceText'. JSON configurations can only pass string patterns, so they can never replace every occurrence with replaceString. Separately, replaceText with an empty searchPattern inserts the replacement between every character.

**Failure scenario.** For 'a-b-c' with pattern '-' and replacement '+', replaceString gives 'a+b-c' and replaceText gives 'a+b+c' (confirmed). replaceText {textContent:'abc', searchPattern:''} gives 'a+b+c' with replacementCount 2 (confirmed).

**Evidence.**

```ts
replace-string.ts:53 `operationData.result = textContent.replace(pattern, replacement);` vs replace-text.ts:86-88 `const parts = textContent.split(searchPattern); count = parts.length - 1; newText = parts.join(replacement);`
```

**Fix.** In replaceString, when `pattern` is a string, use `textContent.split(pattern).join(replacement)` to match replaceText exactly (all occurrences, literal replacement). Alternatively use `textContent.replaceAll(pattern, () => replacement)`. Keep `replace` for RegExp patterns, where the regex flags and `$` substitution are expected. Changing the output for multi-match strings is a behaviour change, so add a CHANGELOG entry. If that change is not wanted, rewrite the doc comment, then rerun `npm run opers-metadata` so the generated description updates. The new comment should say that only the first occurrence is replaced for string patterns, that `$` patterns are expanded, and should drop the "alias for replaceText" wording. Separately, make replaceText (and replaceString, if it adopts split/join) throw `replaceText: searchPattern must not be empty` when given an empty string pattern.

Original suggestion: Use replaceAll for string patterns, and pass the replacement as a function to avoid $-pattern expansion (or document it). Otherwise, correct the doc comment so it no longer calls the operation an alias. Have replaceText throw on an empty searchPattern.

**Verifier.** I traced the code and the claim holds. In src/operation/replace-string.ts:53, `textContent.replace(pattern, replacement)` with a string `pattern` uses native String.prototype.replace. That replaces only the first occurrence and expands `$&`, `$1`, `$$` and similar in `replacement`. In src/operation/replace-text.ts:86-88, string patterns go through split/join, which replaces every occurrence and inserts the replacement literally. So for 'a-b-c' with '-' and '+', replaceString gives 'a+b-c' and replaceText gives 'a+b+c'.  I found nothing that rules this out: (1) No guard or conversion turns a configured string into a global RegExp. The only `new RegExp` outside tests is in validate-form.ts:126. JSON cannot hold a RegExp, and the generated metadata (src/operation/metadata/replace-string.ts) shows `pattern` as 'string|RegExp', so a JSON configuration can only send a string. That means it can never replace every occurrence with replaceString. (2) The behaviour is not documented as intended. The TypeDoc and the generated metadata description both say "Replaces occurrences of a pattern ... (alias for replaceText for string context)". That promises replaceText semantics, which the code does not deliver. The @example has a single match, so it does not settle the question either way. (3) No test covers it. src/test/unit/operation/replace-string.spec.ts only tests single-match cases ('Hello World' -> 'Hello Universe', and a non-global /\d+/) plus the null-textContent throw. There is no multi-occurrence test and no `$`-pattern test.  The empty-pattern claim also holds: in replaceText, 'abc'.split('') gives ['a','b','c'], so join('+') returns 'a+b+c' and the count is 2. No guard rejects an empty searchPattern, and no test uses one.  Severity stays low. Nothing crashes and engine state is not corrupted. The result is just wrong text from a valid configuration, and you can work around it with replaceText.

**Repro test.** `given textContent 'a-b-c' with string pattern '-' and replacement '+', when replaceString is applied, then result is 'a+b+c' like replaceText`

### 62. [low] getGlobals(name) structuredClones class instances: throws or strips methods

[deep-copy.ts:65](src/operation/helper/deep-copy.ts#L65) · area: operations-b · reported severity: low · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** deepCopy keeps jQuery objects, DOM nodes and functions by reference, but sends every other non-plain object to structuredClone. A class instance with function-valued fields, or with nested Maps or arrays of functions, makes structuredClone throw DataCloneError. Examples are a controller from getControllerInstance (BaseController keeps listener removers) and an action from requestAction (its eventbus holds handler functions). Instances without functions come back as plain objects without their prototype methods. getGlobals(name), which addGlobalsToOperation uses, deep-copies every read.

**Failure scenario.** getControllerInstance -> setGlobalData {propertyNames:['controllerInstance']} -> in another action, addGlobalsToOperation {propertyNames:['controllerInstance']} fails with DataCloneError instead of returning the controller.

**Evidence.**

```ts
deep-copy.ts:65-67: `const copy = copyFunction(value); seen.set(value, copy); return copy;` reached for any value where isPlainObject is false; globals.ts:10 `return value && value !== cache ? deepCopy(value) : value;`
```

**Fix.** In copyValue (deep-copy.ts), call structuredClone only for known cloneable built-ins: Date, RegExp, Map, Set, ArrayBuffer and typed arrays, Error. Return every other non-plain object (any value whose prototype is not Object.prototype or null) by reference, the same way isReferenceOnly already returns jQuery and Node values. Add tests to deep-copy.spec.ts and globals.spec.ts that store a BaseController subclass with a registered listener and assert that getGlobals(name) returns the same instance.

Original suggestion: Only clone plain objects, arrays and known value types (Date, Map, Set, RegExp). Keep other class instances by reference, as deepCopy already does for jQuery and Node.

**Verifier.** I traced the scenario through the real code and found nothing that blocks it.  1. Storing the controller. setGlobalData (src/operation/set-global-data.ts) passes the raw operation-data values to setGlobals. That stores the controller instance by reference, with no copy.  2. Reading it back. addGlobalsToOperation (src/operation/add-globals-to-operation.ts:29) calls getGlobals(name) for each name in `globalProperties`. The report says `propertyNames` here, but the operation's parameter is `globalProperties`. That slip does not change the result. In globals.ts:10, any truthy named value that is not the cache itself goes through deepCopy.  3. The copy. In deep-copy.ts, copyValue lets a controller pass every earlier branch:    - It is not a primitive or a function.    - isReferenceOnly is false: it has no `jquery` string and is not a Node.    - It is not an array.    - isPlainObject is false, because its prototype is LabelController.prototype or similar.    So it reaches line 65, `copyFunction(value)`, which is structuredClone.  4. What structuredClone does with a class instance. I checked this in Node:    - An instance whose fields hold an array of functions throws DataCloneError.    - An instance with no function fields comes back as a plain object with Object.prototype, and `init` is undefined.     An attached BaseController keeps `eventListeners: TEventbusRemover[]`, an array of remover functions, so it throws. A fresh controller from getControllerInstance has an empty `eventListeners` array, so it loses its methods instead. Either way the controller that comes back is broken.  What I looked for and did not find: - No guard elsewhere stops this. action.ts copyConfiguredOperationData only deep-copies operation data from the configuration, so it is not involved. - The deepCopy doc comment says other objects are structured-cloned. It lists Date, Map, Set and RegExp, and nothing says class instances are meant to be stripped. - No test covers class instances. deep-copy.spec.ts covers only plain data, functions, jQuery/DOM, cycles and `__proto__`. globals.spec.ts covers only jQuery, functions and plain data.  The state is reachable from a valid JSON configuration. Either of these works: - getControllerFromElement, or getControllerInstance, then setGlobalData {propertyNames:['controllerInstance']}. - Later, addGlobalsToOperation {globalProperties:['controllerInstance']}.  Why severity stays low: - Sharing controller instances through globals is an unusual pattern. Controllers are normally fetched and used inside one action. - There is a workaround. `$globaldata.controllerInstance` property-chain resolution calls getGlobals() with no name, which returns the cache itself without copying, so the instance comes back by reference. - Other class instances, such as an action from requestAction, are affected the same way. These are niche too.

**Repro test.** `given a controller instance with registered listener removers stored via setGlobalData, when addGlobalsToOperation reads it with globalProperties ['controllerInstance'], then the same controller instance is returned without throwing DataCloneError`

### 63. [low] $globaldata chains return live references; setGlobalData stores by reference

[resolve-external-property-chain.ts:46](src/operation/helper/resolve-external-property-chain.ts#L46) · area: operations-b · reported severity: low · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** getGlobals(name) deliberately returns a copy so that operations cannot change the shared store. '$globaldata.*' resolution instead calls getGlobals() with no name, which returns the raw cache, and hands out live references into it. setGlobalData also stores operation-data objects by reference (_setGlobals assigns directly). In-place edits of operation data therefore change the global store without any setGlobalData, depending on which path read the value.

**Failure scenario.** setGlobalData stores settings {theme:'light'}. A later action runs setOperationData {properties:{cfg:'$globaldata.settings'}} -> setData {properties:{'$operationdata.cfg.theme':'dark'}}. The global settings.theme is now 'dark', while addGlobalsToOperation callers in the same action received independent copies.

**Evidence.**

```ts
resolve-external-property-chain.ts:46 `return getPropertyChainValue(propNames, getGlobals(), operationName);`; globals.ts:9-10 `const value = name ? cache[name] : cache; return value && value !== cache ? deepCopy(value) : value;` and :14 `Object.entries(newValues).forEach(([name, value]) => (cache[name] = value));`
```

**Fix.** In resolve-external-property-chain.ts, deep-copy the value a $globaldata chain resolves to: `return deepCopy(getPropertyChainValue(propNames, getGlobals(), operationName));`. In globals.ts _setGlobals, store copies with `cache[name] = deepCopy(value)`. Leave the raw-cache return of getGlobals() unchanged, because setData's '$globaldata.*' write target, setGlobal, removeGlobal and log rely on it.

Original suggestion: Choose one semantic. To keep copy-on-read, deepCopy the value that a $globaldata chain resolves to, and copy values in _setGlobals. Otherwise, drop the copy in getGlobals(name) and document that globals are shared references.

**Verifier.** I traced the scenario through the real code and it holds. A temporary Vitest spec reproduced it, and I have deleted that spec.  1. setGlobalData (src/operation/set-global-data.ts) passes the values filtered from operation data to setGlobals. _setGlobals (globals.ts:14) stores them with `cache[name] = value`, so the store keeps a reference, not a copy. 2. setOperationData calls resolvePropertyValues. That deep-copies the `properties` object, which only holds the string '$globaldata.settings'. It then sets `copy[key] = resolvePropertyChain(value)`. resolveExternalPropertyChain:46 resolves the chain against `getGlobals()` with no name. In _getGlobals, `value === cache` skips the deepCopy, so the raw cache comes back and `cfg` becomes the live `cache.settings` object. 3. Running operation data is not deep-copied between operations. Action.copyConfiguredOperationData (action.ts ~260) copies only the configured data, not the data passed along the chain, so `cfg` stays a live reference. 4. setData '$operationdata.cfg.theme' calls getPropertyChainValue(['cfg'], operationData), which returns that same live object, and writes `theme = 'dark'` into it.  Result: getGlobals('settings').theme is 'dark' without any write to $globaldata. The repro test failed with "expected 'dark' to be 'light'".  No guard prevents this. I found nothing documenting shared-reference semantics, and the copy in getGlobals(name) plus addGlobalsToOperation's "copied" docs suggest copy-on-read was intended. No existing test covers it: resolve-external-property-chain.spec and set-data.spec only test primitive values and pollution guards.  Severity stays low. Triggering it needs an object-valued global read through a $globaldata chain and then edited in place. setData can already write `$globaldata.*` on purpose, so the impact is confusing aliasing, not corruption of engine state.

**Repro test.** `given setGlobalData stored settings {theme:'light'}, when setOperationData resolves cfg from '$globaldata.settings' and setData writes '$operationdata.cfg.theme' = 'dark', then getGlobals('settings').theme is still 'light'`

### 64. [low] saveToStorage stores the string 'undefined', which loadFromStorage then cannot parse

[save-to-storage.ts:64](src/operation/save-to-storage.ts#L64) · area: operations-b · reported severity: low · verifier: sonnet

**Summary.** JSON.stringify(undefined) returns undefined, and Storage.setItem stores it as the string 'undefined', which is not valid JSON. Both storage operations also accept any storageType string and fall back to localStorage, so a typo such as 'sesion' silently writes to persistent storage.

**Failure scenario.** saveToStorage {key:'k', storageType:'local'} where value is missing (it was erased or never set by the previous operation) stores 'undefined'. A later loadFromStorage {key:'k'} throws 'loadFromStorage: failed to parse stored value for key "k"'. storageType:'Session' writes to localStorage.

**Evidence.**

```ts
save-to-storage.ts:63-65: `const storage = storageType === 'session' ? sessionStorage : localStorage; const serialized = JSON.stringify(value); storage.setItem(key, serialized);`
```

**Fix.** In saveToStorage, throw `saveToStorage: value is required (key "${key}")` when value === undefined. Calling removeItem would quietly hide an upstream mistake, so throwing is the better choice. Add a shared guard in both operations: if storageType !== 'local' && storageType !== 'session', throw `<op>: storageType must be 'local' or 'session', got "${storageType}"`. Also update or remove the existing 'should default to localStorage when storageType not specified' spec, since it conflicts with `@required`. In loadFromStorage, pass the parse error as `{cause}`.

Original suggestion: Throw `saveToStorage: value is required` when value === undefined, or call removeItem. Validate storageType against 'local' | 'session' in both operations and throw on anything else. Pass the parse error as `cause` in loadFromStorage.
